https://jingjing12110.github.io/SE-MMAgents Email: jingjingjiang2017@gmail.com Abstract Recent work shows that multimodal agents increasingly self-improve without recurring human annotation.
However, each self-improvement mechanism is designed and evaluated within a single scenario, leaving its preconditions unverified under cross-scenario transfer.
This survey investigates the structural source of asymmetric mechanism transfer across four scenarios: multimodal reasoning, multimodal generation, GUI and Web navigation, and embodied manipulation.
We first formalize the self-evolving multimodal agent by four criteria and a differentia of self-reference, and adopt the three-stage self-evolving loop of experience generation, feedback acquisition, and update consolidation as our analytical framework to position the scenarios along ceilings on rollout count and feedback fidelity.
Comparing self-evolving with fixed loops within each scenario separates the limitations that the scenario imposes from those inherent in the self-evolving design.
We then recast each mechanism as a trade-off that raises a scarce factor by presupposing a task-structural resource, such as output equivalence or checkable outcomes, and judge it portable, conditional, blocked, or redundant across scenarios.
We attribute the asymmetry to divergent combinations of scarce factors and resources across opposite transfer directions, and identify the matched-budget measurements that could overturn these still untested verdicts. I Introduction Recent advances in multimodal foundation models have fueled an explosive proliferation of multimodal agents [ 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 ] , spawning a flourishing spectrum of self-evolution techniques across multimodal reasoning [ 5 , 6 , 7 , 8 ] , multimodal generation [ 9 , 10 , 11 , 12 , 13 ] , GUI and Web navigation [ 14 , 15 , 16 , 17 ] , and embodied manipulation [ 18 , 19 , 20 , 21 ] .
Despite strong scenario-specific gains, existing self-evolving methods are designed and evaluated within single scenarios, and their mechanisms do not transfer equally well in both directions of a scenario pair.
For example, majority voting derives label-free rewards for reasoning tasks from consistency across sampled answers [ 22 , 5 , 23 ] , yet finds no counterpart in embodied manipulation, where action trajectories admit no equivalence check and physical rollouts incur steep execution and reset costs.
Conversely, world models circumvent expensive environmental interactions via imagined rollouts [ 24 , 25 ] , but raise a factor that is not scarce in reasoning, where sampling directly from the policy costs little.
Beneath this directional breakdown in mechanism transferability, which suggests a cross-scenario asymmetry , also lies a shared self-evolving loop across diverse tasks.
Specifically, across all four scenarios, self-evolving agents share the same cycle, i.e. , generating exploratory experience, acquiring evaluative feedback, and consolidating persistent updates.
This survey investigates the structural source of cross-scenario asymmetry by characterizing this shared loop and explaining why the two directions of a scenario pair meet different obstacles to transfer. TABLE I : Comparison with related surveys. Scenarios indicates domains covered in dedicated sections: MR (multimodal reasoning), MG (multimodal generation), GW (GUI and Web navigation), and EM (embodied manipulation), or General if scenario-agnostic. Loop decomposition denotes the stages or components formalized by each survey, where EG, FA, and UC represent experience generation, feedback acquisition, and update consolidation, respectively (– indicates none). [ 39 ] arXiv’26 General Formation × \times management tier Multimodal agent memory Ours MR, MG, GW, EM EG / FA / UC Cross-scenario portability However, as summarized in Table I , existing work leaves this cross-scenario asymmetry unaddressed.
Primary studies benchmark exclusively within specific scenarios [ 40 , 20 ] , while prior surveys either overlook the self-evolving loop as an analytical object [ 1 , 2 , 3 , 41 , 42 ] or formalize it as scenario-agnostic [ 28 , 31 , 33 ] .
Even the closest related work [ 29 ] attributes verifiable feedback and retraining costs to domain-level paradigms, not to specific mechanisms.
To fill this gap, we first formalize the surveyed object through a genus and a differentia .
The genus comprises autonomous learning loops satisfying four criteria: ( i ) autonomy , where the agent operates without human intervention across goals, actions, feedback, and updates post-initialization; ( ii ) freshness , where experience is generated under the current policy; ( iii ) durability , where updates persist beyond the episode boundary; and ( iv ) recurrence , where the loop iterates across cycles.
The differentia, self-reference , requires the agent to realize at least one component of its own loop: the goal sampler, the feedback function, or the evolution operator.
Loops in the genus lacking self-reference, termed fixed loops , serve as controls across all scenarios to disentangle scenario-imposed constraints from the intrinsic effects of self-evolution. To locate where a mechanism can transfer, we adopt the three-stage self-evolving loop as our analytical framework, decomposing each cycle into experience generation, feedback acquisition, and update consolidation.
We formalize each stage’s bottleneck through two governing factors: ( i ) rollout count and information gain, ( ii ) feedback fidelity and credit localization, and ( iii ) retention and generalization.
Each scenario imposes ceilings on the rollouts affordable per cycle ( K ¯ \bar{K} ) and the feedback fidelity attainable by admissible evaluators ( F ¯ \bar{F} ), which span a two-dimensional positioning matrix .
In this matrix, multimodal reasoning is situated where both ceilings are high, multimodal generation where F ¯ \bar{F} is low, GUI and Web navigation where K ¯ \bar{K} is low, and embodied manipulation where both are low, with simulation raising both ceilings.
Comparing self-evolving and fixed loops within each cell further separates the limits imposed by scenario ceilings from those incurred by self-evolving design, e.g. , replacing answer keys with sampled consensus in reasoning [ 22 , 5 ] .
The matrix thus identifies which ceilings bind in each scenario, but alone cannot determine whether a given mechanism is portable. Building on this matrix, we formulate each self-evolving mechanism as a trade-off , in which the agent raises a scarce factor by replacing a fixed component with its own records, proposals, judgments, or memory writes, at the price of presupposing a task-structural resource, such as output equivalence or checkable outcomes.
In a target scenario, a mechanism is thus portable if the target holds this resource, conditional if it holds the resource only for a sub-family or after adaptation, blocked if the outcome to be judged is unobservable, and redundant if the raised factor is not scarce.
We attribute the cross-scenario asymmetry structurally to the different combinations of scarce factors and resources that the two directions of a scenario pair encounter: e.g. , voting finds no output equivalence in manipulation, whereas world models raise a factor that is not scarce in reasoning.
These verdicts, inferred from single-scenario evidence and not yet tested, define testable preconditions, not empirical guarantees.
A gain over a budget-matched ablation without self-evolution would overturn any verdict that predicts no benefit from an unadapted transfer, which turns these predictions into a concrete measurement agenda. This paper makes four contributions, the first two to the survey and the last two to the analysis. ✜ A condition-based definition and paradigm demarcation. We operationalize autonomy and the differentia through a provenance matrix that records which party realizes each loop component, and apply the definition to sixteen neighboring paradigms, of which five fall outside the genus, two never satisfy the differentia, five are divided by it, and four are self-evolving by definition. ✜ A stage-resolved review and positioning matrix. We review each scenario through the three-stage self-evolving loop, place the four scenarios in the positioning matrix, contrast self-evolving with fixed loops in each cell, and trace five degradation modes along the cross-cycle paths through which they propagate. ✜ Directed portability across scenario pairs. We recast the mechanisms of all four scenarios as trade-offs and assign each a portability verdict for every target scenario, yielding a structural account of the cross-scenario asymmetry. ✜ A falsifiable measurement agenda. We review the evaluation of task-solving capability, self-evolving capability, and judges, and identify the measurements whose absence leaves untestable three claims, i.e. , that a gain stems from self-evolution, that a scenario occupies its assigned position, and that the verdicts hold. The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. Sec. II defines the surveyed object, and Sec. III develops the three-stage loop and the positioning matrix. Secs. IV , V , VI and VII review the four scenarios, and Sec. VIII derives the portability verdicts. Finally, Sec. IX treats evaluation, and Secs. X and XI discuss open challenges and conclude. II Preliminaries Definition 1 ( Multimodal Agent ) A multimodal agent over observation, action, and goal spaces ( 𝒪 , 𝒰 , 𝒢 ) (\mathcal{O},\mathcal{U},\mathcal{G}) is specified by the four-component tuple 𝒜 = ( Φ θ , ℳ , 𝒯 , ℐ ) , \mathcal{A}\;=\;\bigl(\,\Phi_{\theta},\;\mathcal{M},\;\mathcal{T},\;\mathcal{I}\,\bigr), whose components are the following. • Φ θ : ( ℝ d ) ∗ → Δ ⁡ ( 𝒱 ~ ⊔ 𝒵 latent ) \Phi_{\theta}:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{*}\to\Delta(\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}\sqcup\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{latent}}) is the core foundation model , a next-step distribution over a discrete multimodal vocabulary 𝒱 ~ \widetilde{\mathcal{V}} and a latent space 𝒵 latent ⊆ ℝ d ′ \mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{latent}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}} for diffusion or flow-matching heads, whose iterated sampling defines the sequence-level distribution Φ θ ⋆ \Phi_{\theta}^{\star} . • ℳ = ℳ short × ℳ long \mathcal{M}\!=\!\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{short}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{long}} is the memory , an episode-scoped buffer ℳ short ⊆ ( ℝ d ) ∗ \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{short}}\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{*} and a persistent key-indexed store ℳ long ⊆ ( ℝ d × ( ℝ d ) ∗ ) ∗ \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{long}}\subseteq\bigl(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{*}\bigr)^{*} . A write operator Ψ : ℳ × 𝒪 × 𝒰 → ℳ short \Psi:\mathcal{M}\!\times\!\mathcal{O}\!\times\!\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{short}} updates the buffer within an episode, and only the evolution operator of Sec. II-A writes ℳ long \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{long}} . • 𝒯 = { η j : 𝒳 j → Δ ⁡ ( 𝒴 j ) } j = 1 | 𝒯 | \mathcal{T}\!=\!\{\eta_{j}:\mathcal{X}_{j}\to\Delta(\mathcal{Y}_{j})\}_{j=1}^{|\mathcal{T}|} is the tool set , a registry of typed callable interfaces. Tool actions 𝒰 tool \mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{tool}} are calls of a fixed form, a tool name with its arguments, 𝒯 \mathcal{T} fixes which calls execute, and their execution effects enter the transition P P of Sec. II . • ℐ = ( ϕ , ρ ) \mathcal{I}\!=\!(\phi,\rho) is the interaction interface , a prompt assembler ϕ : 𝒪 × ℳ × 𝒢 → ( ℝ d ) ∗ \phi:\mathcal{O}\!\times\!\mathcal{M}\!\times\!\mathcal{G}\!\to\!(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{*} that embeds heterogeneous context into one sequence and an action parser ρ : ( 𝒱 ~ ⊔ 𝒵 latent ) ∗ → 𝒰 \rho:(\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}\sqcup\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{latent}})^{*}\!\to\mathcal{U} that decodes a generated sequence into an action. The agent is multimodal when 𝒪 \mathcal{O} or 𝒰 \mathcal{U} contains a non-textual channel that Φ θ \Phi_{\theta} encodes or decodes natively. Definition 2 ( Multimodal Environment ) Under a goal g ∈ 𝒢 g\in\mathcal{G} , the environment of a multimodal agent is the goal-conditioned POMDP 𝒫 g = ( 𝒮 , 𝒪 , 𝒰 , 𝒢 , μ 0 , P , Z , R , γ ) , \mathcal{P}_{g}\;=\;\bigl(\,\mathcal{S},\;\mathcal{O},\;\mathcal{U},\;\mathcal{G},\;\mu_{0},\;P,\;Z,\;R,\;\gamma\,\bigr), with latent state space 𝒮 \mathcal{S} , initial-state distribution μ 0 ( ⋅ ∣ g ) ∈ Δ ( 𝒮 ) \mu_{0}(\cdot\mid g)\in\Delta(\mathcal{S}) , transition kernel P : 𝒮 × 𝒰 → Δ ⁡ ( 𝒮 ) P:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{U}\to\Delta(\mathcal{S}) that includes tool-execution effects, observation function Z : 𝒮 → Δ ⁡ ( 𝒪 ) Z:\mathcal{S}\to\Delta(\mathcal{O}) , goal-conditioned reward R : 𝒮 × 𝒰 × 𝒢 → ℝ R:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{G}\to\mathbb{R} , which we treat as the idealized task objective, and discount γ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \gamma\in[0,1] , which the horizon cap T max T_{\max} keeps well defined at γ = 1 \gamma=1 . The interface spaces are multimodal. • 𝒪 = 𝒪 vis ⊥ × 𝒪 txt ⊥ × 𝒪 aud ⊥ × 𝒪 prop ⊥ \mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\bot}\times\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{txt}}^{\bot}\times\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{aud}}^{\bot}\times\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{prop}}^{\bot} ranges over visual, textual, auditory, and proprioceptive or tactile channels, where 𝒳 ⊥ := 𝒳 ∪ { ⊥ } \mathcal{X}^{\bot}:=\mathcal{X}\cup\{\bot\} and ⊥ \bot marks a channel absent at a step. • 𝒰 = 𝒰 text ⊔ 𝒰 gen ⊔ 𝒰 act ⊔ { stop } \mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{text}}\sqcup\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{gen}}\sqcup\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{act}}\sqcup\{\textsc{stop}\} admits one category per step, text generation, artifact synthesis, or digital and physical intervention 𝒰 act = 𝒰 tool ⊔ 𝒰 gui ⊔ 𝒰 motor \mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{act}}=\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{tool}}\sqcup\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{gui}}\sqcup\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{motor}} . • 𝒢 \mathcal{G} contains multi-channel goals, such as a language constraint paired with a target image or pose, together with any task parameter, such as an initial scene, that a goal fixes. Conceptually, a multimodal agent is a goal-directed decision-making system that interacts with an external environment through a closed perception-cognition-action loop over heterogeneous observation and scenario-specific action spaces [ 43 ] .
We restrict the scope to agents whose cognitive core is a large (multimodal) foundation model [ 44 , 45 , 46 , 47 , 48 , 49 , 50 , 51 ] extended via interface [ 52 , 53 , 54 ] , memory [ 55 ] , and tools [ 56 , 57 , 58 , 59 ] , excluding architectures lacking such a core, such as world-model planners and task-specific visuomotor policies.
Since the environment supplies perceptions, accepts actions, and specifies goals, we adopt multimodal agent-environment interaction as our formal scaffold.
To systematically diagnose the cross-scenario asymmetry, we unfold this loop across two temporal scales: a micro-scale capturing step-by-step behaviors within a single episode, and a macro-scale tracking the cycle-by-cycle evolution of the agent’s own components over long-term experiences.
The resulting dual-scale dynamics are formalized under goal-conditioned partially observable Markov decision processes (POMDPs) [ 60 , 61 ] .
On this scaffold, we define the surveyed object across four levels, from the static agent and environment through the learning loop and the autonomous learning loop to the self-evolving loop. II-A Multimodal Agent We formulate the static architecture of a multimodal agent as a four-component tuple 𝒜 \mathcal{A} ( Sec. II ) and its environment as a goal-conditioned, modality-decomposed POMDP 𝒫 g \mathcal{P}_{g} ( Sec. II ).
The multimodality condition of Sec. II requires Φ θ \Phi_{\theta} to directly encode or decode a non-textual channel without intermediate text conversion, such as screen pixels, camera frames, generated images or videos, or low-level motor commands.
Under this criterion, an agent perceiving a web page solely through its HTML or accessibility tree is text-only despite rendered visual content. Definition 3 ( Self-Evolving Multimodal Agent ) Let 𝒜 k = ( Φ θ k , ℳ k , 𝒯 k , ℐ k ) \mathcal{A}_{k}=(\Phi_{\theta_{k}},\mathcal{M}_{k},\mathcal{T}_{k},\mathcal{I}_{k}) denote the agent at cycle k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} . A self-evolving multimodal agent is specified by the triple ℰ 𝒜 = ( 𝒜 0 , V ν , f ζ ) , \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{A}}\;=\;\bigl(\,\mathcal{A}_{0},\;V_{\nu},\;f_{\zeta}\,\bigr), whose components are the following. • 𝒜 0 ∈ 𝔸 mm \mathcal{A}_{0}\in\mathbb{A}^{\mathrm{mm}} is the initial agent , with 𝔸 mm \mathbb{A}^{\mathrm{mm}} the set of configurations of Sec. II . • V ν : ( 𝔻 mm ) ∗ × ( 𝔸 mm ) ∗ → ( 𝕐 mm ) ∗ V_{\nu}:(\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{mm}})^{*}\times(\mathbb{A}^{\mathrm{mm}})^{*}\to(\mathbb{Y}^{\mathrm{mm}})^{*} is the feedback function with configuration ν \nu . It maps a batch of goal-trajectory pairs ( g , τ ) ∈ 𝔻 mm (g,\tau)\in\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{mm}} , with τ \tau distributed as in Eq. 3 , together with the agent history 𝒜 0 : k \mathcal{A}_{0:k} , to feedback in 𝕐 mm \mathbb{Y}^{\mathrm{mm}} , which subsumes scalar rewards, preferences, and step-level, token-level, or structured critiques, and we write V ν ​ ( 𝒟 k ) V_{\nu}(\mathcal{D}_{k}) for V ν ( 𝒟 k , 𝒜 0 : k ) V_{\nu}(\mathcal{D}_{k},\mathcal{A}_{0:k}) . It is agent-independent when the preference it induces over trajectories that share a goal depends only on those trajectories and on artifacts fixed before the loop other than copies of the agent’s model, and it reads the current agent when that preference depends on 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} , through the agent’s model or through other samples of its current policy. • f ζ : 𝔸 mm × ℍ × ( 𝔻 mm ) ∗ × ( 𝕐 mm ) ∗ → 𝔸 mm f_{\zeta}:\mathbb{A}^{\mathrm{mm}}\times\mathbb{H}\times(\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{mm}})^{*}\times(\mathbb{Y}^{\mathrm{mm}})^{*}\to\mathbb{A}^{\mathrm{mm}} is the evolution operator with configuration ζ \zeta , which writes the update of a cycle into the agent tuple and reads the history ℋ k \mathcal{H}_{k} of Sec. II-A when it replays earlier experience. A proposer or evaluator that the agent system trains belongs to Φ θ \Phi_{\theta} , which places it under the updates of f ζ f_{\zeta} and makes σ ω \sigma_{\omega} and V ν V_{\nu} depend on it through 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} . The criteria 𝒞 1 \mathcal{C}_{1} to 𝒞 4 \mathcal{C}_{4} and the differentia below decide whether the loop of the triple with the environment of Sec. II-A is an autonomous learning loop and whether that loop is self-evolving, and only a triple whose loop passes all five tests is a self-evolving multimodal agent. Definition 4 ( Multimodal Macro-Environment ) Let ℋ k = ( 𝒜 0 : k , 𝒟 0 : k − 1 , V ν ( 𝒟 0 : k − 1 ) ) ∈ ℍ \mathcal{H}_{k}\!=\!(\mathcal{A}_{0:k},\mathcal{D}_{0:k-1},V_{\nu}(\mathcal{D}_{0:k-1}))\in\mathbb{H} denote the history of agents, trajectories, and realized scores at the start of cycle k k . The multimodal macro-environment is the triple ℰ ℰ = ( { 𝒫 g } g ∈ 𝒢 , σ ω , μ 𝒢 ) , \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{E}}\;=\;\bigl(\,\{\mathcal{P}_{g}\}_{g\in\mathcal{G}},\;\sigma_{\omega},\;\mu_{\mathcal{G}}\,\bigr), whose components are the following. • { 𝒫 g } \{\mathcal{P}_{g}\} is a task-indexed family of POMDPs of Sec. II that share 𝒮 \mathcal{S} , 𝒪 \mathcal{O} , 𝒰 \mathcal{U} , P P , Z Z , and R R , in which the task argument g g fixes the goal and may fix the initial-state distribution or a generated scene. Environment synthesis therefore acts through g g , whereas drift of P P acts on the loop from outside. • σ ω : ℕ × ℍ → Δ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) \sigma_{\omega}:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{H}\to\Delta(\mathcal{G}) is the goal sampler with configuration ω \omega , which draws the goals of cycle k k from σ ω ​ ( k , ℋ k ) \sigma_{\omega}(k,\mathcal{H}_{k}) . It is stationary when it ignores ( k , ℋ k ) (k,\mathcal{H}_{k}) , scheduled when it depends on k k alone, as a difficulty order fixed before the loop does, and adaptive when it reads ℋ k \mathcal{H}_{k} , as a success-conditioned curriculum does. Placing σ ω \sigma_{\omega} in the macro-environment locates where goals enter the loop, whereas the provenance matrix of Sec. II-C records who realizes it. • μ 𝒢 ∈ Δ ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) \mu_{\mathcal{G}}\in\Delta(\mathcal{G}) is a fixed evaluation distribution outside the control of σ ω \sigma_{\omega} , which measures performance through J ⁡ ( 𝒜 , μ 𝒢 ) := 𝔼 g ∼ μ 𝒢 ​ J ​ ( 𝒜 , g ) J(\mathcal{A};\mu_{\mathcal{G}}):=\mathbb{E}_{g\sim\mu_{\mathcal{G}}}J(\mathcal{A};g) . Micro-Scale Interaction Loop. Within one episode, shown in the micro-scale panel of Figure 1 , the agent acts under a configuration 𝒜 \mathcal{A} with its persistent components ( Φ θ , ℳ long , 𝒯 , ℐ ) (\Phi_{\theta},\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{long}},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{I}) held fixed, leaving only the short-term buffer ℳ short \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{short}} to evolve from initial state m 0 m_{0} .
The episode starts from s 0 ∼ μ 0 ( ⋅ ∣ g ) s_{0}\sim\mu_{0}(\cdot\mid g) , runs over t ∈ { 0 , … , T − 1 } t\in\{0,\dots,T-1\} , and terminates upon a stop action or horizon limit T max T_{\max} , yielding a random stopping time T ≤ T max T\leq T_{\max} .
Each step passes through the environment kernels and agent components: s t → 𝑍 o t → ϕ ⁡ ( ⋅ , m t , g ) x t → Φ θ ⋆ y t → 𝜌 u t → 𝑃 s t + 1 , s_{t}\xrightarrow{Z}o_{t}\xrightarrow{\phi(\cdot,m_{t},g)}x_{t}\xrightarrow{\Phi_{\theta}^{\star}}y_{t}\xrightarrow{\rho}u_{t}\xrightarrow{P}s_{t+1}, (1) where ϕ \phi and ρ \rho are deterministic, Z Z , Φ θ ⋆ \Phi_{\theta}^{\star} , and P P are stochastic, and the buffer updates as m t + 1 = Ψ ⁡ ( m t , o t , u t ) m_{t+1}=\Psi(m_{t},o_{t},u_{t}) .
The fixed argument ℳ long \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{long}} in ϕ \phi is suppressed per episode.
This synchronous discrete-time chain abstracts away the gap between decision and control frequencies, which is immaterial for text actions but adds per-rollout cost under real-time embodied control.
Over the micro-history h t = ( o 0 : t , u 0 : t − 1 ) h_{t}=(o_{0:t},u_{0:t-1}) , the agent induces the policy π 𝒜 = ρ ♯ ​ Φ θ ⋆ \pi_{\mathcal{A}}=\rho_{\sharp}\Phi_{\theta}^{\star} , π 𝒜 ​ ( u t | h t , g ) = Φ θ ⋆ ​ ( ρ − 1 ​ ( u t ) | ϕ ⁡ ( o t , m t , g ) ) , \pi_{\mathcal{A}}(u_{t}|h_{t},g)\!=\!\Phi_{\theta}^{\star}\!\bigl(\rho^{-1}(u_{t})\,\big|\,\phi(o_{t},m_{t},g)\bigr), (2) where conditioning on h t h_{t} suffices, fixed ( 𝒜 , m 0 ) (\mathcal{A},m_{0}) and update Ψ \Psi uniquely determining m t m_{t} from h t h_{t} .
Compounded with environment transitions, this policy induces a trajectory distribution p g π 𝒜 ​ ( τ ) p^{\pi_{\mathcal{A}}}_{g}(\tau) over τ = ( s 0 : T , o 0 : T − 1 , u 0 : T − 1 ) \tau=(s_{0:T},\,o_{0:T-1},\,u_{0:T-1}) : p g π 𝒜 ​ ( τ ) = μ 0 ​ ( s 0 ∣ g ) ​ ∏ t = 0 T − 1 Z ⁡ ( o t | s t ) ​ π 𝒜 ​ ( u t | h t , g ) ​ P ​ ( s t + 1 | s t , u t ) . p^{\pi_{\mathcal{A}}}_{g}(\tau)\!=\!\mu_{0}(s_{0}\mid g)\!\!\prod_{t=0}^{T-1}\!\!Z(o_{t}|s_{t})\pi_{\mathcal{A}}(u_{t}|h_{t},g)P(s_{t+1}|s_{t},u_{t}). (3) Goal-Conditioned Expected Return. The loop induces the goal-conditioned expected return J ⁡ ( 𝒜 , g ) = 𝔼 τ ∼ p g π 𝒜 ​ [ ∑ t = 0 T − 1 γ t ​ R ​ ( s t , u t , g ) ] , J(\mathcal{A};\,g)\!=\!\mathbb{E}_{\tau\,\sim\,p^{\pi_{\mathcal{A}}}_{g}}\!\left[\,\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\gamma^{t}\,R(s_{t},\,u_{t},\,g)\,\right], (4) a performance functional of 𝒜 \mathcal{A} , not a quantity optimized within an episode.
Self-evolution modifies 𝒜 \mathcal{A} between episodes, as the macro-scale loop below formalizes. II-B Self-Evolving Multimodal Agent Informally, a self-evolving multimodal agent improves its persistent components across cycles without human input post-initialization, learns from experience generated under its own policy, and internally realizes at least one improvement driver: selecting its goals, deriving its feedback, or authoring its updates.
We formalize the agent and its environment as the triples ℰ 𝒜 \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{A}} ( Sec. II-A ) and ℰ ℰ \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{E}} ( Sec. II-A ). 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} 𝒫 g \mathcal{P}_{g} u t u_{t} o t o_{t} T max T_{\max} ℳ short \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{short}} ( 𝒜 k , ℋ k ) (\mathcal{A}_{k},\mathcal{H}_{k}) σ ω \sigma_{\omega} V ν V_{\nu} f ζ f_{\zeta} Φ θ \Phi_{\theta} ℳ long \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{long}} 𝒯 \mathcal{T} ℐ \mathcal{I} ℋ k \mathcal{H}_{k} σ ω \sigma_{\omega} 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} 𝒈 k \boldsymbol{g}_{k} 𝒟 k \mathcal{D}_{k} V ν ​ ( 𝒟 k ) V_{\nu}(\mathcal{D}_{k}) 𝒜 k + 1 , ℋ k + 1 \mathcal{A}_{k+1},\,\mathcal{H}_{k+1} k k 𝒞 1 \mathcal{C}_{1} 𝒞 1 \mathcal{C}_{1} 𝒞 1 \mathcal{C}_{1} 𝒞 3 \mathcal{C}_{3} 𝒞 1 \mathcal{C}_{1} 𝒞 2 \mathcal{C}_{2} 𝒞 4 \mathcal{C}_{4} 𝒞 i \mathcal{C}_{i} Fig. 1 : Dual-scale learning loop and its defining conditions. Within an episode (green), 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} acts in 𝒫 g \mathcal{P}_{g} with its persistent components fixed.
Across episodes (blue), each cycle samples goals with σ ω \sigma_{\omega} , scores the resulting batch 𝒟 k \mathcal{D}_{k} with V ν V_{\nu} , and updates the agent with f ζ f_{\zeta} ( Eq. 5 ).
Blue tags mark where each genus criterion applies, and orange SR tags mark the meta-operators, at least one of which a self-evolving agent must realize. Macro-Scale Learning Loop. Across episodes, the macro-scale frame ( Figure 1 ) wraps interactions into agent-updating cycles.
Starting from 𝒜 0 \mathcal{A}_{0} , each cycle k k passes through goal sampling, rollout, evaluation, and update: ℋ k → σ ω 𝒈 k → p 𝒈 k π 𝒜 k 𝒟 k → V ν V ν ​ ( 𝒟 k ) → f ζ 𝒜 k + 1 , \mathcal{H}_{k}\xrightarrow{\sigma_{\omega}}\boldsymbol{g}_{k}\xrightarrow{p^{\pi_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}}_{\boldsymbol{g}_{k}}}\mathcal{D}_{k}\xrightarrow{V_{\nu}}V_{\nu}(\mathcal{D}_{k})\xrightarrow{f_{\zeta}}\mathcal{A}_{k+1}, (5) where 𝒈 k \boldsymbol{g}_{k} denotes the goal batch drawn from σ ω ​ ( k , ℋ k ) \sigma_{\omega}(k,\mathcal{H}_{k}) with support 𝒢 k ⊆ 𝒢 \mathcal{G}_{k}\!\subseteq\!\mathcal{G} , rollout law p 𝒈 k π 𝒜 k p^{\pi_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}}_{\boldsymbol{g}_{k}} applies p g π 𝒜 k p^{\pi_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}}_{g} across each g ∈ 𝒈 k g\in\boldsymbol{g}_{k} , and 𝒟 k \mathcal{D}_{k} aggregates the resulting goal-trajectory pairs (with single goals corresponding to unit batch size).
The history then extends to ℋ k + 1 = ( 𝒜 0 : k + 1 , 𝒟 0 : k , V ν ( 𝒟 0 : k ) ) \mathcal{H}_{k+1}=(\mathcal{A}_{0:k+1},\mathcal{D}_{0:k},V_{\nu}(\mathcal{D}_{0:k})) , recording realized scores, stochastic evaluator outputs being non-recoverable from trajectories alone and failure-conditioned samplers conditioning directly on realized values.
We designate this recursive mapping a learning loop .
The term denotes the cross-cycle structure without presupposing monotonic gains in J ⁡ ( 𝒜 k , μ 𝒢 ) J(\mathcal{A}_{k};\mu_{\mathcal{G}}) , and excludes within-episode adaptation ( e.g. , in-context learning), which remains confined to ℳ short \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{short}} .
A cycle is one iteration of this mapping.
When training proceeds in explicit rounds (each regenerating tasks, evaluator states, or rollouts via the antecedent agent before optimization), a cycle corresponds to one round, whose internal optimizer steps form within-cycle optimization under f ζ f_{\zeta} .
In round-free regimes, such as online reinforcement learning drawing fresh rollouts at every optimizer step, each step executes the full mapping as a degenerate cycle . Meta-Operators and Substrate. We designate σ ω \sigma_{\omega} , V ν V_{\nu} , and f ζ f_{\zeta} the three meta-operators of the loop, each acting on whole episodes at the macro scale.
Across multimodal scenarios, the goal sampler draws from a fixed task pool [ 62 ] , poses questions about unlabeled images [ 5 ] , reselects GUI tasks by historical success [ 17 ] , or synthesizes simulated scenes [ 63 ] .
The feedback function verifies discrete answers [ 64 ] , inspects generated images via the internal understanding pathway [ 10 ] , queries a vision-language judge on the final screen [ 14 ] , or evaluates simulator success predicates [ 62 ] .
The evolution operator updates Φ θ \Phi_{\theta} via policy gradient [ 65 ] or writes lessons, skills, and memory entries [ 66 , 67 , 68 ] . Auxiliary agents enter either as self-play opponents sampled via the task argument ( Sec. II-A ) [ 7 ] or as learned proposer-solver roles instantiated within Φ θ \Phi_{\theta} [ 22 ] .
The substrate denotes the persistent components that f ζ f_{\zeta} modifies across cycles [ 28 , 31 ] . Specifically, a parametric loop updates Φ θ \Phi_{\theta} alone, a non-parametric loop modifies only ℳ long \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{long}} , 𝒯 \mathcal{T} , or ℐ \mathcal{I} under fixed θ \theta (training-free), and a mixed loop alters both [ 69 , 70 ] . Expected Evaluation Return. We evaluate a learning loop at each cycle via its expected return under the evaluation distribution μ 𝒢 \mu_{\mathcal{G}} , which lies outside the control of σ ω \sigma_{\omega} to prevent samplers from inflating performance through easier goals.
With cycles counted in rounds by some methods and in optimizer steps by others, n n cycles span horizons of different lengths, and the return is therefore reported per cycle. With 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} itself random, depending on the goals, rollouts, and feedback recorded in ℋ k \mathcal{H}_{k} , the expectation at cycle k k also ranges over this history, 𝒥 k = 𝔼 ℋ k , g ∼ μ 𝒢 ​ [ J ⁡ ( 𝒜 k , g ) ] , k ∈ ℕ . \mathcal{J}_{k}\!=\!\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{H}_{k},\,g\,\sim\,\mu_{\mathcal{G}}}\!\left[\,J(\mathcal{A}_{k};\,g)\,\right],\quad k\in\mathbb{N}. (6) This sequence evaluates against the idealized reward R R ( Eq. 4 ), whereas the loop ascends the proxy V ν V_{\nu} . II-C Defining Conditions of Self-Evolving Loops To distinguish it from general learning loops, we formalize the self-evolving loop through a genus of four operational criteria and a differentia of self-reference. The Genus: Autonomous Learning Loops. We adopt four criteria to bound the genus of autonomous learning loops.
Autonomy bars human input across all three meta-operators and rollout actions, whereas freshness, durability, and recurrence respectively exclude static datasets, episodic buffer writes, and uniterated single-shot updates: 𝒞 1 \mathcal{C}_{1} Autonomy. Autonomy requires the loop to operate without human intervention across its meta-operators and action stream post-initialization.
Formally, at every cycle k ≥ 0 k\!\geq\!0 , the goals 𝒈 k \boldsymbol{g}_{k} , the signal V ν ​ ( 𝒟 k ) V_{\nu}(\mathcal{D}_{k}) , and the update of f ζ f_{\zeta} consume no annotation, verdict, or approval added by a human while the loop runs, and no human-issued action, such as a teleoperated correction, enters 𝒟 k \mathcal{D}_{k} .
Artifacts fixed prior to cycle 0 0 (task sets, verification checkers, answer keys, external evaluators and judges, or optimizers) constitute the bootstrap. Human resets modifying 𝒫 g \mathcal{P}_{g} and not the learning loop itself, autonomy does not imply reset-free execution [ 71 ] , and reset overhead is documented separately. 𝒞 2 \mathcal{C}_{2} Freshness. Freshness requires per-cycle experience to be drawn from the agent’s current policy, not from a fixed dataset or an external stream.
Formally, the cycle- k k distribution depends on 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} through π 𝒜 k \pi_{\mathcal{A}_{k}} , admitting on-policy sampling 𝒟 k ∼ p 𝒈 k π 𝒜 k \mathcal{D}_{k}\!\sim\!p^{\pi_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}}_{\boldsymbol{g}_{k}} and asynchronous variants whose behavior policy lags 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} by a bounded number of updates, but excluding any agent-independent p static p_{\mathrm{static}} [ 31 ] .
Experience collected once by 𝒜 0 \mathcal{A}_{0} and reused in later cycles fails the criterion. Generating on-policy rollouts, however, satisfies freshness without constituting self-reference. 𝒞 3 \mathcal{C}_{3} Durability. Durability requires the update to alter the agent beyond the episode boundary.
Formally, 𝒜 k + 1 = f ζ ​ ( 𝒜 k , ℋ k , 𝒟 k , V ν ​ ( 𝒟 k ) ) \mathcal{A}_{k+1}\!=\!f_{\zeta}(\mathcal{A}_{k},\mathcal{H}_{k},\mathcal{D}_{k},V_{\nu}(\mathcal{D}_{k})) must modify persistent components ( Φ θ k \Phi_{\theta_{k}} , long-term memory ℳ long ⊆ ℳ k \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{long}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{k} , toolset 𝒯 k \mathcal{T}_{k} , or interface ℐ k \mathcal{I}_{k} ), thereby altering induced policy π 𝒜 k + 1 \pi_{\mathcal{A}_{k+1}} on at least one goal.
This excludes adaptation confined to the within-episode buffer ℳ short \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{short}} and updates restricted to auxiliary proposers or evaluators, which belong to Φ θ \Phi_{\theta} yet occupy parameters that π 𝒜 \pi_{\mathcal{A}} does not read, while admitting memory writes, tool additions, and prompt mutations even without any gradient descent [ 31 , 34 ] . 𝒞 4 \mathcal{C}_{4} Recurrence. Recurrence requires the loop to close across cycles instead of terminating after a single update.
Formally, the map 𝒜 k ↦ 𝒜 k + 1 \mathcal{A}_{k}\!\mapsto\!\mathcal{A}_{k+1} of 𝒞 3 \mathcal{C}_{3} is iterated over more than one cycle, whether rounds or degenerate cycles, and each output becomes the next cycle’s agent, not a branch discarded after evaluation.
This excludes single-pass optimization pipelines ( 𝒜 0 ↦ 𝒜 1 \mathcal{A}_{0}\!\mapsto\!\mathcal{A}_{1} ), even if compliant with 𝒞 1 \mathcal{C}_{1} through 𝒞 3 \mathcal{C}_{3} .
The criterion presupposes 𝒞 3 \mathcal{C}_{3} and, being satisfied by every multi-step online method, discriminates loops only in round-based regimes. Promptbreeder [ 109 ] ; STOP [ 110 ] ; Gödel Agent [ 111 ] The Differentia: Self-Reference. The four criteria constrain how the loop runs, not who realizes it, a loop being able to satisfy 𝒞 1 \mathcal{C}_{1} – 𝒞 4 \mathcal{C}_{4} while every meta-operator is realized outside the agent.
The differentia, self-reference (SR), separates self-evolving loops by requiring the agent to internally realize at least one evolutionary meta-operator, marked by SR tags in Figure 1 . 1 1 1 Our criteria adapt those of [ 31 , 28 , 34 ] and treat updated components as a descriptive substrate, but enforce a narrower scope.
While prior work categorizes learning from environmental or rule-based rewards as reward-based self-evolution [ 31 ] or subsumes reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards on fixed datasets [ 28 ] , self-reference excludes these regimes whenever the agent realizes zero meta-operators.
It does not require co-evolving environment dynamics [ 34 ] , evolving inter-agent topologies [ 28 ] , or lifelong operation ( k → ∞ k\to\infty ). Self-reference and the meta-operator clause of autonomy both ask who realizes each meta-operator, and we therefore record their evaluation for a method m m in the provenance matrix 𝚪 ⁡ ( m ) ∈ { 0 , 1 } 3 × 4 \boldsymbol{\Gamma}(m)\in\{0,1\}^{3\times 4} .
Its rows index ( σ ω , V ν , f ζ ) (\sigma_{\omega},V_{\nu},f_{\zeta}) and columns span four provenance classes: runtime human input ( h ), bootstrap artifacts fixed prior to initialization ( b ), environment/task generation ( e ), and the agent ( a ), with Γ i ​ j ​ ( m ) = 1 \Gamma_{ij}(m)=1 when class j j participates in realizing meta-operator i i (rows admit multiple ones). Both conditions evaluate over single columns: 𝒞 1 : 𝚪 ⁡ ( m ) ​ 𝐞 h = 𝟎 , SR : 𝚪 ⁡ ( m ) ​ 𝐞 a ≠ 𝟎 , \mathcal{C}_{1}:\ \boldsymbol{\Gamma}(m)\,\mathbf{e}_{\text{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}}=\mathbf{0},\qquad\mathrm{SR}:\ \boldsymbol{\Gamma}(m)\,\mathbf{e}_{\text{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{a}}}}}}\neq\mathbf{0}, (7) where 𝐞 h \mathbf{e}_{\text{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}} and 𝐞 a \mathbf{e}_{\text{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{a}}}}}} select the h and a columns, rendering the split between b and e descriptive.
For example, SimpleVLA-RL [ 62 ] realizes all three meta-operators externally, whereas VisPlay [ 5 ] samples goals via an internal Questioner within Φ θ \Phi_{\theta} and derives feedback from votes over its own answers, the two matrices differing in the σ ω \sigma_{\omega} and V ν V_{\nu} rows: 𝚪 SimpleVLA-RL = ( 0 1 0 0 0 1 0 0 0 1 0 0 ) h b e a 𝚪 VisPlay = ( 0 1 0 1 0 0 0 1 0 1 0 0 ) h b e a . \boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\text{SimpleVLA-RL}}=\overset{\text{\makebox[5.0pt]{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}\hskip 6.0pt\makebox[5.0pt]{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{b}}}}}\hskip 6.0pt\makebox[5.0pt]{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{e}}}}}\hskip 6.0pt\makebox[5.0pt]{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{a}}}}}}}{\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0&0\\
0&1&0&0\\
0&1&0&0\end{pmatrix}}\quad\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\text{VisPlay}}=\overset{\text{\makebox[5.0pt]{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{h}}}}}\hskip 6.0pt\makebox[5.0pt]{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{b}}}}}\hskip 6.0pt\makebox[5.0pt]{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{e}}}}}\hskip 6.0pt\makebox[5.0pt]{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{a}}}}}}}{\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0&1\\
0&0&0&1\\
0&1&0&0\end{pmatrix}}. (8) The a entry is tested under row-specific criteria.
The goal-sampler row is marked when σ ω ​ ( k , ℋ k ) \sigma_{\omega}(k,\mathcal{H}_{k}) conditions on the agent or its history, as with learned proposers, success-dependent curriculum rules, or self-play opponents drawn via the task argument ( Sec. II-A ); post-hoc filtering of tied goals leaves subsequent draws unconditioned and is excluded.
The feedback row is marked when V ν V_{\nu} depends on the agent, through the active policy (self-judges, consensus voting over rollouts, or reward models refitted on trajectories) or frozen snapshots (including the initial agent 𝒜 0 \mathcal{A}_{0} ); group-relative normalization and per-goal monotone transforms add no dependence.
Snapshots count because shared model lineage preserves priors, and model-based evaluators reportedly favor their own generations [ 112 ] , a bias plausibly transmitted to descendant policies.
The evolution-operator row is marked when the agent model, its replica, or an internally trained component writes update content beyond raw rollouts, such as a lesson, skill, memory entry, or relabeled goal. This row tests writer provenance, not functional dependence, every f ζ f_{\zeta} depending trivially on the updated 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} .
Promptbreeder [ 109 ] , which rewrites its own improvement mechanism, is a special case of an a entry in this row.
Beyond membership, an a entry tracking the current agent couples successive cycles through shared model parameters or history-conditioned goal rules, whereas a frozen snapshot couples none. Fixed Loops and Scope. An autonomous learning loop with self-reference is self-evolving , whereas one lacking it is fixed-loop , completing the membership test in Sec. II-A .
Because the subsequent three-stage decomposition, its six factors, the two ceilings, and cross-cycle coupling are defined over autonomous learning loops, fixed-loop methods serve there as evidence characterizing a scenario’s operating point, whereas scenario chapters review self-evolving loops, reporting their provenance rows and design variables.
A method can have a entries in several rows ( e.g. , proposer-solver methods realize both the goal sampler and the feedback function [ 22 , 5 ] ). 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} 𝒜 k + 1 \mathcal{A}_{k+1} σ ω \sigma_{\omega} 𝒬 k EG = K k ⋅ 𝔼 ⁡ [ I 𝒜 k ] \mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{EG}}_{k}=K_{k}\cdot\mathbb{E}[I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}] V ν V_{\nu} 𝒬 k FA = ( 2 ​ F ν − 1 ) ⋅ 𝔼 ⁡ [ L ν ] \mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{FA}}_{k}=(2F_{\nu}{-}1)\cdot\mathbb{E}[L_{\nu}] R R f ζ f_{\zeta} 𝒬 k UC = S ζ ⋅ G ζ \mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{UC}}_{k}=S_{\zeta}\cdot G_{\zeta} K ¯ \bar{K} I 𝒜 k I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}} F ¯ \bar{F} L ν L_{\nu} S ζ S_{\zeta} G ζ G_{\zeta} 𝒟 k \mathcal{D}_{k} V ν ​ ( 𝒟 k ) V_{\nu}(\mathcal{D}_{k}) σ ω \sigma_{\omega} ℋ k + 1 \mathcal{H}_{k+1} 𝒈 k + 1 \boldsymbol{g}_{k+1} Fig. 2 : The three-stage self-evolving loop and its six factors. Each stage centers on one meta-operator, and its effectiveness 𝒬 k \mathcal{Q}_{k} is the product of two factors.
The filled ceilings K ¯ \bar{K} and F ¯ \bar{F} serve as the axes of the positioning matrix ( Figure 3 ).
The goal edge, fed by the trajectories and scores of cycle k k , reaches σ ω \sigma_{\omega} only when it is adaptive, and extensions of the agent edge into σ ω \sigma_{\omega} , FA, or UC are not drawn. II-D Demarcation from Established Paradigms The term self-evolving shares vocabulary with several established paradigms, and recent surveys draw its boundary at different points [ 28 , 31 , 34 , 113 , 114 ] . Table II compares adjacent paradigms; contested boundaries hinge on the a entry.
In the goal-sampler row, discarding tied groups within a batch adds no a entry [ 62 ] , whereas allocating subsequent rollouts by task success qualifies [ 115 ] .
In the evolution-operator row, the test is who writes the update: a separate fixed model ( e.g. , GPT-4o guiding GPT-3.5-Turbo in TextGrad) keeps the loop fixed [ 104 , 102 ] , whereas the acting model itself, as with ExpeL’s insights [ 66 ] or STaR’s rationales for failed instances [ 92 ] , establishes self-evolution.
Reflexion [ 116 ] , where the acting model preserves self-reflections across episodic trials, qualifies as self-evolving, although its reflections, written for one task, improve no other task.
Anti-forgetting mechanisms of continual learning map onto the evolution operator f ζ f_{\zeta} , whereas classical curriculum samplers such as POET [ 117 ] , PLR and other regret-based environment designers [ 118 , 119 , 120 ] , goal-proposing teachers [ 121 , 122 ] , and expert-iteration and AlphaZero self-play [ 123 , 124 ] remain outside our foundation-model scope. III The Three-Stage Self-Evolving Loop We adopt the three-stage self-evolving loop as our analytical framework, decomposing each cycle of an autonomous learning loop into experience generation, feedback acquisition, and update consolidation.
Fixed and self-evolving loops across all four scenarios sharing this structure, we differentiate them by where the bottleneck binds instead of by topology, treating rollout cost and feedback fidelity as bounds on viable mechanisms.
We first formalize the three stages and their governing factors, then position the four scenarios within a positioning matrix spanned by these bounds, and trace how the stages couple across cycles. III-A Loop Decomposition As shown in Figure 2 , we factor each cycle into three scenario-invariant stages, each organized around one meta-operator ( σ ω \sigma_{\omega} , V ν V_{\nu} , f ζ f_{\zeta} ).
This decomposition applies across the entire genus of autonomous learning loops and treats auxiliary operations, such as credit assignment or data filtering, as refinements within a stage, not as additional stages. Stage 1 Experience Generation (EG): the goal sampler σ ω \sigma_{\omega} draws the goal batch 𝒈 k \boldsymbol{g}_{k} , and the current agent 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} rolls out in 𝒫 g \mathcal{P}_{g} for each goal g ∈ 𝒈 k g\in\boldsymbol{g}_{k} , producing the trajectory batch 𝒟 k \mathcal{D}_{k} .
Through the quantity and informativeness of 𝒟 k \mathcal{D}_{k} , the only trajectories FA and UC act on, EG bounds how fast and how broadly the loop can evolve ( speed and breadth ). Stage 2 Feedback Acquisition (FA): the feedback function V ν V_{\nu} maps 𝒟 k \mathcal{D}_{k} , together with the agent history 𝒜 0 : k \mathcal{A}_{0:k} when V ν V_{\nu} reads the agent, to scores, preferences, or critiques sourced from the environment, the bootstrap, or the agent itself.
FA is the loop’s only source of information about the idealized task reward R R ( the R R -aligned direction ). Stage 3 Update Consolidation (UC): the evolution operator f ζ f_{\zeta} computes an update from ( 𝒜 k , ℋ k , 𝒟 k , V ν ​ ( 𝒟 k ) ) (\mathcal{A}_{k},\mathcal{H}_{k},\mathcal{D}_{k},V_{\nu}(\mathcal{D}_{k})) and writes it into at least one persistent component of 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} , producing the next-cycle agent 𝒜 k + 1 \mathcal{A}_{k+1} .
UC determines whether an update persists across later cycles and generalizes beyond the goals that produced it ( retention and generalization ). The six factors originate at the two scales of Figure 1 .
The per-episode cost c ⁡ ( τ ) c(\tau) governing K ¯ \bar{K} and action localization L ν L_{\nu} arise within the micro-scale interaction loop, whereas information gain, evaluator fidelity F ν F_{\nu} , and consolidation factors ( S ζ S_{\zeta} , G ζ G_{\zeta} ) emerge from the macro-scale learning loop, the positioning matrix thereby pairing a micro-scale axis with a macro-scale one. TABLE III : Asymmetric substitutability of 𝒬 k EG \mathcal{Q}_{k}^{\mathrm{EG}} across four scenarios. Profiling experience generation across the quantity and quality factors from Eq. 9 .
The bottom row highlights the binding bottleneck for each scenario, showing where cheap rollouts saturate and where costly environments constrain scale. II-A .
For each goal g g in the batch 𝒈 k \boldsymbol{g}_{k} drawn from σ ω ​ ( k , ℋ k ) \sigma_{\omega}(k,\mathcal{H}_{k}) , 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} draws K k K_{k} rollouts from p g π 𝒜 k p^{\pi_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}}_{g} and pools them into 𝒟 k \mathcal{D}_{k} .
EG must supply experience of sufficient scale and informativeness under finite compute, wall-clock, and safety budgets, a constraint on quantity and one on quality.
We summarize this bottleneck through an effective-experience decomposition, 𝒬 k EG := K k ⋅ 𝔼 g ∼ σ ω ​ ( k , ℋ k ) ​ [ I 𝒜 k ​ ( g ) ] , \mathcal{Q}_{k}^{\mathrm{EG}}\;:=\;K_{k}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{g\sim\sigma_{\omega}(k,\mathcal{H}_{k})}\!\left[I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}(g)\right], (9) where K k K_{k} is the number of per-goal rollouts in cycle k k , and I 𝒜 k ​ ( g ) := Var τ ∼ p g π ​ [ ret g ​ ( τ ) ] I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}(g)\!:=\!\mathrm{Var}_{\tau\sim p^{\pi}_{g}}[\mathrm{ret}_{g}(\tau)] is the outcome variance of g g . Here, ret g ​ ( τ ) = ∑ t γ t ​ R ​ ( s t , u t , g ) \mathrm{ret}_{g}(\tau)\!=\!\sum_{t}\gamma^{t}R(s_{t},u_{t},g) denotes the return of τ \tau , and p g π p^{\pi}_{g} abbreviates p g π 𝒜 k p^{\pi_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}}_{g} .
This variance, a goal-level proxy for the learnable information gain [ 131 ] , vanishes on goals that 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} has mastered or never solves, where GRPO-style group-relative updates receive zero advantage under an R R -aligned evaluator.
Under a binary terminal reward with γ = 1 \gamma\!=\!1 , it reduces to p g ​ ( 1 − p g ) p_{g}(1-p_{g}) , which difficulty-targeting rewards and filters track via an estimated success probability p g p_{g} .
Defined on the idealized reward R R of Eq. 4 , the proxy renders 𝒬 k EG \mathcal{Q}_{k}^{\mathrm{EG}} diagnostic, and it exposes two failure modes. As K k K_{k} shrinks, too few per-goal rollouts remain to contrast success with failure. As 𝔼 ⁡ [ I 𝒜 k ] \mathbb{E}[I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}] approaches zero, the sampled goals are mastered or unreachable.
Concretely, K k K_{k} is bounded by per-rollout interaction cost and action recoverability , while 𝔼 ⁡ [ I 𝒜 k ] \mathbb{E}[I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}] is governed by the diversity and information gain targeted by σ ω \sigma_{\omega} and by the interaction mode realizing p g π 𝒜 k p^{\pi_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}}_{g} ( Table III ). Quantity Constraint. Interaction cost and action recoverability govern K k K_{k} via a single per-rollout cost c ⁡ ( τ ) c(\tau) , eliminating the need for a separate term for actions that resist reverting, resetting, or safe repetition.
Specifically, c ⁡ ( τ ) c(\tau) absorbs the time, compute, synchronization, and reset cost of one rollout, with the reset term penalizing both environment restoration and expected damage from failed trajectories.
Under a fixed per-goal budget B B , the rollout count satisfies the affordability ceiling K k ≤ K ¯ := B / 𝔼 τ ​ [ c ⁡ ( τ ) ] K_{k}\!\leq\!\bar{K}\!:=\!B/\mathbb{E}_{\tau}[c(\tau)] , where the expectation runs over episodes of the target task family at the horizon cap T max T_{\max} , not over the rollouts of a particular policy, which lets K ¯ \bar{K} reflect task structure and not policy choice.
When c ⁡ ( τ ) c(\tau) is small, group-sampling operators such as self-consistency [ 80 , 132 ] or GRPO [ 65 , 133 , 134 ] sample a group of rollouts per goal at negligible marginal cost.
As c ⁡ ( τ ) c(\tau) grows, the bottleneck shifts from theoretical execution feasibility to the budget of exploratory failures the system can absorb. Quality Constraint. Even with a large K k K_{k} , a trajectory set is unproductive if its goals are already mastered or still unreachable for 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} , leaving EG viable only while 𝔼 ⁡ [ I 𝒜 k ] > 0 \mathbb{E}[I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}]\!>\!0 . For instance, self-play [ 131 ] stops improving once its synthetic pipeline no longer supplies learnable information gain. Diversity and information gain determines whether σ ω \sigma_{\omega} draws goals with nonzero I 𝒜 k I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}} . Sampling schemes escalate in intervention from random sampling [ 65 ] through curriculum mining over past failures [ 135 , 17 ] and active task selection [ 136 , 137 ] to environment synthesis [ 63 , 138 , 139 , 140 , 141 ] .
Random sampling keeps σ ω \sigma_{\omega} stationary, whereas curriculum mining and active selection make it adaptive. Environment synthesis acts through the task argument g g of Sec. II-A and is adaptive only when the generator reads the agent’s record.
Only an adaptive sampler conditions the next goal draw on the agent’s record and places an a entry in the goal-sampler row. Interaction mode fixes what evidence each trajectory can expose. Single-pass generation and tool-augmented reasoning yield single-action episodes, whereas digital and embodied environments expose multi-step state transitions whose informativeness depends on platform dynamics, reset conditions, and observability. Asymmetric Substitutability. The product in Eq. 9 enables mutual compensation between its two factors, locating the cross-scenario asymmetry in the cost of raising each factor.
Raising K k K_{k} offsets low information gain only when rollouts are cheap, parallelizable, and recoverable and feedback retains contrast as group size grows. Consensus rewards losing contrast beyond small groups [ 22 ] , the loop elsewhere must instead raise 𝔼 ⁡ [ I 𝒜 k ] \mathbb{E}[I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}] via curriculum mining [ 115 , 17 ] , active exploration [ 5 , 18 ] , demonstrations at initialization [ 19 ] , or synthetic environments [ 6 , 63 , 138 ] .
Equal values of K k K_{k} therefore do not imply identical bottlenecks.
Multimodal generation and GUI/Web navigation report overlapping group sizes ( Table III ), yet generation samples cheap, reversible passes below K ¯ \bar{K} and is bounded by 𝔼 ⁡ [ I 𝒜 k ] \mathbb{E}[I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}] over a fixed prompt pool, whereas navigation incurs latency-bound episodes and is bounded by K ¯ \bar{K} itself.
Cheap but uninformative rollouts saturate the loop, whereas informative but costly rollouts throttle its scaling. MR MG GW EM ① Fidelity factor F ν F_{\nu} : how faithfully does V ν ​ ( 𝒟 k ) V_{\nu}(\mathcal{D}_{k}) order trajectories by ret g \mathrm{ret}_{g} ? ✜Signal source (provenance) majority vote a , self-judgment a , computed answers e self-rewards a , same-model judges a , external judges b task checkers b , VLM judges b , co-evolving RMs a , same-model judges a episode outcomes e , progress heads and same-model judges a , VLM judges b ✜Channel coverage full (discrete answer) often perceptual only full on screen, partial for backend state full in simulation, visual only on hardware ✜Gaming resistance under f ζ f_{\zeta} high for computed answers, untested for votes low [ 9 , 129 ] untested beyond downstream success [ 142 ] low without judge oversight [ 143 ] ② Localization factor L ν L_{\nu} : how finely can credit be apportioned along τ \tau ? ✜Temporal granularity episode = = step ( T = 1 T\!=\!1 without tool calls) episode ( T = 1 T\!=\!1 if single-pass) episode (judges), step (co-evolving RMs) episode (outcomes), step (progress heads) ✜Credit-assignment cost none ( T = 1 T\!=\!1 ) none if single-pass, low otherwise co-trained step RMs [ 135 , 144 ] demonstration-trained progress heads [ 19 , 145 ] Primary bottleneck of 𝒬 k FA \mathcal{Q}_{k}^{\mathrm{FA}} Realized F ν ( k ) F_{\nu}^{(k)} Fidelity ceiling F ¯ \bar{F} Localization Both, on hardware III-C Stage 2: Feedback Acquisition (FA) FA maps the trajectory batch 𝒟 k \mathcal{D}_{k} of each cycle to the feedback V ν ​ ( 𝒟 k ) ∈ ( 𝕐 mm ) ∗ V_{\nu}(\mathcal{D}_{k})\!\in\!(\mathbb{Y}^{\mathrm{mm}})^{*} , the scores, preferences, critiques, or structured judgments that direct f ζ f_{\zeta} .
Any scoring, ranking, filtering, or best-of- N N selection by V ν V_{\nu} belongs to FA even when interleaved with generation, whereas V ν V_{\nu} -independent filtering such as deduplication remains in EG.
FA must supply feedback that is aligned with the intended objective R R and localized at an actionable grain without recurring human supervision ( 𝒞 1 \mathcal{C}_{1} ), a constraint on fidelity and one on localization.
We summarize this bottleneck through an effective-feedback decomposition, 𝒬 k FA := ( 2 ​ F ν ​ ( 𝒟 k , R , 𝒈 k ) − 1 ) ⋅ 𝔼 τ ∼ p ^ 𝒟 k ​ [ L ν ​ ( τ ) ] , \mathcal{Q}_{k}^{\mathrm{FA}}\;:=\;\bigl(2F_{\nu}(\mathcal{D}_{k};R,\boldsymbol{g}_{k})-1\bigr)\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\widehat{p}_{\mathcal{D}_{k}}}\!\left[L_{\nu}(\tau)\right], (10) where p ^ 𝒟 k \widehat{p}_{\mathcal{D}_{k}} is the empirical measure on 𝒟 k \mathcal{D}_{k} , F ν ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] F_{\nu}\!\in\![0,1] is the rate at which the ordering V ν ​ ( 𝒟 k ) V_{\nu}(\mathcal{D}_{k}) induces on same-goal trajectory pairs agrees with their ordering by ret g \mathrm{ret}_{g} , and L ν ​ ( τ ) ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] L_{\nu}(\tau)\!\in\!(0,1] is the fraction of environment actions along τ \tau to which V ν V_{\nu} can assign credit.
Here, pairs tied under either ordering are excluded, and F ν := 1 / 2 F_{\nu}\!:=\!1/2 if none remains.
This pairwise form covers scalar, pairwise, and ranked feedback alike, and 2 ​ F ν − 1 2F_{\nu}-1 is the Goodman-Kruskal rank correlation of the two orderings, zero for a random evaluator and negative for a reversing one.
Like I 𝒜 k I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}} , F ν F_{\nu} is defined on R R , which makes 𝒬 k FA \mathcal{Q}_{k}^{\mathrm{FA}} diagnostic, and it exposes two failure modes. As F ν F_{\nu} falls toward 1 / 2 1/2 , f ζ f_{\zeta} follows an uninformative proxy, and below 1 / 2 1/2 it optimizes against R R . As L ν L_{\nu} shrinks, a correct signal becomes too coarse to guide improvement.
Concretely, F ν F_{\nu} is bounded by the signal source and gaming resistance of V ν V_{\nu} , while L ν L_{\nu} is governed by the temporal granularity and credit-assignment cost of its scores ( Table IV ). Fidelity Constraint. Signal source and gaming resistance act on F ν F_{\nu} at different levels: the signal source fixes the evaluator configurations Ξ V \Xi_{V} a scenario admits, hence the attainable-fidelity ceiling F ¯ := sup ν ∈ Ξ V F ν \bar{F}\!:=\!\sup_{\nu\in\Xi_{V}}F_{\nu} , whereas gaming resistance governs how much of it survives repeated f ζ f_{\zeta} updates.
Where a closed-form success condition or a discrete answer space exists, deterministic instruments such as answers computed by task generation [ 64 , 146 ] , unit tests [ 147 , 148 ] , and physical-constraint rewards [ 18 , 145 , 149 ] sustain high F ν F_{\nu} at modest cost.
Lacking both, evaluators rely on self-rewards [ 9 , 10 , 150 , 151 ] , reward models [ 135 , 144 , 152 , 153 , 154 , 155 , 156 ] , or VLM judges [ 14 , 18 , 157 , 158 ] , and gaming resistance binds [ 9 , 159 ] . Channel coverage further caps F ν F_{\nu} , an instrument leaving unobserved channels of 𝒰 \mathcal{U} unconstrained [ 9 , 18 , 145 ] .
Self-rewards and co-trained reward models read 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} and place an a entry in the feedback row, and their realized fidelity F ν ( k ) F_{\nu}^{(k)} therefore drifts with the policy they score; all others enter as b or e .
Reported audits measure label or verdict accuracy a a , not the pairwise agreement that defines F ν F_{\nu} . Under a binary terminal reward with verdict errors independent across trajectories and symmetric across outcomes, a success-failure pair is ordered correctly with probability a 2 a^{2} and reversed with probability ( 1 − a ) 2 (1-a)^{2} , giving F ν = a 2 / ( a 2 + ( 1 − a ) 2 ) F_{\nu}=a^{2}/(a^{2}+(1-a)^{2}) , which exceeds a a whenever a > 1 / 2 a>1/2 . A reported accuracy thus bounds F ν F_{\nu} from below only under these assumptions, and errors correlated within a goal void the bound.
In multimodal reasoning, program-computed answers stay correct on 94.4% of retained questions [ 64 ] , yet self-generated pseudo-labels degrade across rounds [ 5 , 160 ] , consistent with self-referential evaluators drifting below a high F ¯ \bar{F} , although rising question difficulty confounds these declines.
