arXiv’26 b deployment trials (Sim, Real) – b text and video judges, windowed verification a LoRA adapters, skills, task memory (M, T) – π 0.5 \pi_{0.5} , Gemini 3.8 Flash VII-A EG Bound by Physical Rollouts In embodied manipulation, experience generation draws goals that pair a language instruction with a scene, and each rollout is an episode of camera observations and robot actions, emitted by a vision-language-action policy or by programs that a language model writes over perception and control primitives.
The task families center on tabletop and household manipulation in LIBERO and its variants, ManiSkill, RoboCasa, RoboTwin, Robosuite, VIMA-Bench, and VLABench [ 293 , 295 , 294 , 69 , 300 , 190 ] , extending to memory-dependent tasks in RoboMemArena [ 292 ] and physical tasks on robot arms [ 19 , 21 , 301 , 305 ] .
These goals come from a benchmark suite, a library of scene-feasible tasks, or the agent’s own proposals, and weight-updating loops run groups of episodes in parallel simulators, training-free loops sequential attempts, and hardware loops one episode at a time, with RoboEvolve imagining episodes inside a learned video simulator [ 298 ] . Table XII summarizes the self-evolving methods that satisfy the conditions of Sec. II-C . Quantity and Quality Constraints. Experience generation is bound by both factors on hardware and chiefly by quality in simulation ( Table III ).
On hardware, quantity binds through physical time, human support, and scene recoverability, each real-world experiment of Self-Improving EFMs running about 20 hours with human station resets [ 19 ] and 6.58% of the 456 autonomous episodes of Find Something You Can’t Do ending in a scene that only a human restores [ 21 ] .
Simulation lifts this bound with parallel environments and group sampling [ 62 , 18 , 306 ] , and where it also supplies a success predicate, reinforcement learning needs no agent-derived verifier and appears chiefly in fixed loops [ 287 , 95 , 161 , 307 , 308 ] .
Quality binds on both sides, a long-horizon task beyond the policy’s reach returning p g ​ ( 1 − p g ) ≈ 0 p_{g}(1-p_{g})\approx 0 , and on hardware a persistent workspace leaves only part of a task library feasible after each episode [ 21 ] , each unreachable or infeasible task costing a full physical rollout. Task Choice and World Models. A larger group is affordable only in simulation, which also supplies the success predicate, and loops pull two other levers: task choice raises 𝔼 ⁡ [ I 𝒜 k ] \mathbb{E}[I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}] at a cost paid in verification, and a learned world model buys rollouts at a cost paid in fidelity.
Task choice stays within a library or its edits, Find Something You Can’t Do sampling scene-feasible tasks of low recent success [ 21 ] and Reflection-VLA simplifying failing tasks through edited simulation definitions [ 304 ] .
Self-proposed practice extends this choice [ 302 , 20 , 166 ] and needs a check for each new goal, supplied by simulator predicates, by validation seeds that a sealed judge grades in SEAR [ 20 ] , or by verifiers of the agent’s own model in RATs [ 302 ] .
Controlled returns are reported for both, removing the performance-aware curriculum of Find Something You Can’t Do markedly slowing its real-world improvement [ 21 ] , failure-ranked collection lowering the ManiSkill failure rate of Policy-Level RSI below random collection [ 296 ] , and curious play lifting the RATs executor on LIBERO-PRO to 44.3% against 36.3% without play and 38.3% with random play [ 302 ] .
A learned world model makes rollouts cheap and recoverable but passes its errors to feedback, as in RoboEvolve, which co-trains its planner with a video simulator from unlabeled seed images [ 298 ] , and in World-VLA-Loop, which sets long-horizon suites aside once generated video degrades beyond about 300 frames [ 24 ] . VII-B FA Bound by Real-Robot Fidelity Each cycle hands feedback acquisition an episode of camera frames and actions, together with the simulator state in simulation, and Table XII groups the methods by who supplies the feedback.
Scores from the environment ( e ) come from simulator predicates, execution errors, and termination status [ 290 , 294 , 293 , 292 , 296 , 297 ] .
Scores from the agent ( a ) come from progress heads and value models frozen before reinforcement learning [ 19 , 145 , 299 ] , from a co-trained critic or a reward proxy refit on the policy’s rollouts [ 298 , 25 ] , and from the frozen model that acts and then judges its own trials [ 300 , 190 , 20 , 301 , 302 , 303 ] .
Scores from outside the agent ( b ) come from VLM judges and critics [ 304 , 18 , 69 , 21 , 305 ] .
Weight-updating methods turn scores into rewards or advantages for on- or off-policy reinforcement learning [ 18 , 69 , 19 , 145 , 299 ] , whereas training-free methods turn them into success labels and diagnoses that gate memory, skill, and code writes [ 300 , 290 , 294 ] . Fidelity and Localization Constraints. Feedback acquisition is bound on real robots by fidelity and by a localization whose step-level signals inherit the fidelity of their estimators ( Table IV ), the attainable fidelity being high in simulation and low on hardware.
A simulator predicate checks completion exactly, whereas on a real robot a judge must infer it from images with an accuracy reported between 69% and 100% across judges and test sets, from 100% for the best of five VLM judges on 100 image pairs in Find Something You Can’t Do [ 21 ] to 78.9% for the judge of SOAR on 546 trajectories [ 173 ] and 69% to 75% for that of VideoAgent on 36 videos [ 172 ] .
Cameras show an outcome’s appearance and not contact or force, which judges reading images cannot observe.
Under optimization, SEAR reports agents working around its sealed judge, for instance by reading object geometry from asset files [ 20 ] , and policies trained against VLM judges learn to exploit them [ 143 ] .
Localization comes only from learned estimators, such as progress heads, VLAC critics, and per-step verifiers, which return a signal at every step or sub-goal [ 19 , 145 , 69 , 302 ] , whereas a simulator predicate or a real-robot judge returns one verdict per episode. Dense Feedback from Learned Verifiers. A simulator predicate stays fixed as the policy improves, whereas the learned verifiers that make real-robot feedback dense or cheap incur degradations set by whether they follow the policy.
Progress heads and value models frozen before reinforcement learning cut the agent edge [ 19 , 145 , 299 ] but meet distribution shift on states that the improving policy reaches outside its imitation data, the key challenge Self-Improving EFMs names for its reward model [ 19 ] .
Co-trained critics keep the edge and invite compounding reward hacking, which RoboEvolve counters by binarizing its physical reward terms, citing as stability monotone reward curves that hacking would equally produce [ 298 ] , and MERL by down-weighting low-trust imagined chunks, outperforming a matched control [ 25 ] .
A frozen self-judge opens no path into FA but writes its misjudged trials into the memory or skills it later retrieves [ 300 , 190 , 20 , 301 , 302 ] , unless a sealed judge checks them on validation seeds, a judge that the agents of SEAR still circumvent [ 20 ] .
External judges stay independent of the policy but cost 2.6 to 16.9 s per query in Find Something You Can’t Do [ 21 ] , bounding the rollouts they can score.
Dense feedback on hardware thus rests on fidelity audited within one task distribution, for judges on at most a few hundred samples [ 172 , 21 , 173 ] and for the progress head on target-task demonstrations [ 19 ] . VII-C UC Bound by Generalization Update consolidation in embodied manipulation persists the scored experience in weight updates, training-free writes, or, in four methods, both ( Table XII ).
Weight-updating methods write Φ θ \Phi_{\theta} with an update rule fixed before the loop, through reinforcement learning or fine-tuning on their own successes, RoboEvolve updating its video simulator alongside its planner [ 19 , 304 , 18 , 145 , 21 , 296 , 299 , 25 , 298 ] .
Training-free methods keep the backbone frozen and let the agent’s language model write reflections [ 300 , 301 ] , skills, code, or harnesses [ 290 , 20 , 294 , 302 , 303 , 190 , 289 , 297 ] , or memory of verified behaviors and physical knowledge [ 292 , 293 , 291 ] , over two to 50 rounds, a rollout budget, or one pass of a task stream.
ViSkill [ 295 ] , Agentic-VLA [ 69 ] , and Robo-COP [ 305 ] train the policy alongside skill cards, a memory of adapted weights, or admitted skills, and EmbodiedRSI keeps its VLA frozen while training the policy that manages its memory of code and skills [ 166 ] . Retention and Generalization Constraints. Update consolidation is bound by generalization ( Table V ), success falling with the distance of the test from practice even where gains persist within one benchmark, whereas retention is measured only in fixed loops, self-evolving ones guarding against regression by gates.
The code policies of SEAR fall from 87.8% on new initial states of practiced tasks to 33.3% on new tasks and 28.7% under a new simulator and action interface, and its trained VLAs from 73.2% to 43.1% and 10.2% [ 20 ] .
Gains of written artifacts persist within one benchmark, SHAPER adding 2.5 points on its in-domain VLABench split and 6.0 to 10.0 on splits with unseen targets or task forms [ 190 ] , although its absolute success on the splits with unseen targets falls from 42.5% to 26.0% and 19.5%.
Hardware transfer is tested on small sets, five tasks of 30 trials each in EmbodiedRSI [ 166 ] , eight scenes per task in RoboHarn-Evo [ 291 ] , and 20 tests per task in Robo-COP, which doubles success on only one of three held-out tasks [ 305 ] .
Retention is measured only in the fixed loops CRL-VLA and Simple Recipe [ 95 , 161 ] and approximated in self-evolving loops only by gates against regression, the adoption test of Robo-COP and the risk gate of Policy-Level RSI [ 305 , 296 ] .
As in navigation, backbones confound cross-form comparison: training-free loops mostly run frozen frontier models, weight-updating loops open ones. Tests outside the Writer. Feedback acquisition sets the exposure of consolidation, the two forms of writing differing in whether a test outside the writer checks a write before it persists.
Parametric writes pass on the verifier’s score, inheriting the distribution shift of frozen progress heads [ 19 , 145 , 299 ] and the compounding hacking of co-trained critics [ 298 , 25 ] , except where the adoption test of Robo-COP or the risk gate of Policy-Level RSI adds a test outside the update rule [ 305 , 296 ] .
Non-parametric writes pass tests at different distances from the writer, from held-out seeds and fixed validation sets [ 289 , 190 , 297 ] through execution outcomes [ 290 , 294 , 293 , 292 ] to the writer’s own judgment [ 300 , 20 , 301 , 302 ] .
A write that only its writer checks persists with its errors, one SEAR run saving a self-calibrated placement verifier and finishing at 4.6% against 15.5% and 23.6% for two sibling runs without it [ 20 ] .
A test outside the writer matters even when it checks only task success, the harness of Scale and Selection falling from 40% to 0% over 20 rounds once every revision is accepted, whereas champion-challenger selection raises held-out success from 51% to 67% [ 297 ] .
The farther the test sits from the writer, the more of the retained gain it protects. VII-D Limits and Preconditions Bottleneck Attribution. In embodied manipulation, both ceilings bind fixed and self-evolving loops alike on hardware, self-reference changing who supplies the goal, the score, or the write but not the cost or attainable fidelity of a physical rollout.
Fixed loops meet the same ceilings, training against simulator success [ 62 , 287 ] or letting a separate model write memory or revise a skill library [ 188 , 309 , 310 , 311 ] .
The cost of self-reference lies in what each entry point puts at risk: a success-conditioned choice over a fixed library risks little, only the allocation following the agent [ 21 ] , whereas self-proposed practice moves verification to checks the agent builds [ 20 ] , a co-trained critic lets its bias follow the policy [ 298 , 25 ] , and a frozen model that judges and writes carries one bias into both roles [ 300 , 301 ] .
Task choice and practice offset part of these costs, and only there are controlled gains reported [ 21 , 302 ] .
Beyond these ablations, the costs rest on stage evidence and the provenance in Table XII . Cross-Cycle Degradation. Across cycles, self-reference opens paths along which degradation follows the agent, and the environment path adds the sim-to-real gap.
On the goal side, success-conditioned task choice, simplification, and failure-ranked collection open the goal edge [ 21 , 304 , 296 ] and self-proposed practice extends the agent edge into σ ω \sigma_{\omega} [ 302 , 20 , 166 , 298 ] , both inviting curriculum collapse.
On the feedback side, co-trained critics extend the agent edge into FA, inviting compounding reward hacking [ 298 , 25 ] , and training a planner on videos of its co-trained simulator invites model collapse [ 298 ] , whereas frozen progress heads stay off the agent edge yet admit distribution shift [ 19 ] .
The extension into UC carries erroneous writes [ 20 ] and regression when every checkpoint is deployed, below the frozen policy on three of the four tasks whose first candidate its gate rejected [ 305 ] , while the environment path carries dynamics that loops closed in simulation never score.
Within reported budgets, regression and erroneous writes are documented, whereas curriculum collapse, compounding reward hacking, model collapse, and distribution shift remain inferred. Presupposed Resources. The mechanisms of embodied manipulation presuppose resources that simulation supplies and hardware withholds.
Reinforcement learning against success presupposes simulator success predicates [ 62 ] , held-out gates simulated or deployment trials [ 289 , 305 ] , learned hardware feedback an audited judge [ 21 , 173 ] , and written artifacts a test outside the writer [ 297 , 20 ] . VIII Cross-Scenario Asymmetry This section characterizes the cross-scenario asymmetry, in which the two directions of a scenario pair receive different kinds of portability verdict, a mechanism that applies in one scenario needing an adaptation, being blocked, or becoming redundant in the other while the reverse direction meets a different combination.
We interpret the asymmetry through two properties of each scenario, which factors are scarce there, exogenously or through resources that self-reference gives up, and which task-structural resources and conditions it holds, chiefly output equivalence, checkable outcomes, and outcome observability.
A mechanism is portable on a target that holds the resource it presupposes, conditional where that resource is held only on a sub-family or after an adaptation, blocked where the outcome it judges or must check is unobservable, and redundant where the factor it raises is not scarce.
The account concerns the kind of verdict each direction receives and leaves aside cost differences across scenarios, which the generation-verification gap would set.
Quadrant placements and cost comparisons are inferred from single-scenario evidence, and the verdicts remain untested. VIII-A Exogenous and Endogenous Scarcity A factor is scarce in a scenario where its value limits what a loop gains, a condition that a binding ceiling implies but that a factor without a ceiling can also meet.
Scarcity is exogenous where it limits fixed loops as well and endogenous where self-reference gives up a resource that a fixed loop keeps, two reasons that comparing the self-evolving and fixed loops of each scenario separates ( Secs. IV-D , V-D , VI-D and VII-D ). Exogenous Scarcity. Exogenous scarcity has two sources, the saturation of 𝔼 ⁡ [ I 𝒜 k ] \mathbb{E}[I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}] in every scenario and the low ceilings of three.
We infer the saturation from p g ​ ( 1 − p g ) p_{g}(1-p_{g}) falling near zero where goals are mastered or out of reach.
Generation saturates from above, the fixed loop Flow-GRPO raising the GenEval score of SD3.5-M from 0.63 to 0.95 [ 195 ] , and navigation and manipulation from below, with initial navigation success below 20% [ 16 , 135 ] and physical scenes that afford only part of a task library [ 21 ] .
Unbounded by any ceiling, 𝔼 ⁡ [ I 𝒜 k ] \mathbb{E}[I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}] counts as scarce in every scenario.
Fixed loops meet the low ceilings as well, scoring open-ended images with learned preference models in generation [ 195 , 267 , 312 , 313 , 314 , 315 ] , paying per-step environment latency in navigation [ 269 ] , and facing resets and success evaluation on hardware [ 21 , 188 ] .
Self-reference leaves these ceilings in place, changing who supplies the goal, the score, or the write and with it the form of their cost. Endogenous Scarcity. Endogenous scarcity, the residual bottleneck we place in multimodal reasoning, where neither ceiling is low, arises once loops give up the answer keys that fixed loops keep.
Fixed loops filter rollouts by exact match against pre-collected keys, as in M-STAR [ 152 ] , whereas loops that pose or label their own questions without a program to compute answers label by consensus or self-judgment at a lower realized fidelity F ν ( k ) F_{\nu}^{(k)} .
On 500 shared questions, VQS finds 24% of retained majority-vote labels and 18% of retained model-judge labels wrong against 5.6% of retained program-computed answers [ 64 ] .
Self-posed goals also give up scripts in SCALE [ 284 ] and SEAR [ 20 ] without an endogenous scarcity of fidelity, SCALE’s web end states remaining open to inspection and SEAR’s goals staying in simulation, short of the hardware on which both ceilings bind. TABLE XIII : Trade-offs and degradation of self-evolving mechanisms. Evidence stops short of cross-round measurement for most degradation modes.
Notation: (T) task-structural resource, (M) capability of the agent’s model, and artifact authored before the loop if unmarked; cut path closed by a frozen evaluator. writes wrong verifier saved to memory [ 20 ] MR [ 146 , 225 ] , MG [ 256 , 264 ] , GW [ 162 , 281 ] , EM [ 290 , 20 ] VIII-B Task-Structural Resources Whether a meta-operator that the agent realizes can raise a scarce factor varies across scenarios only with resources that the scenario holds independently of the agent.
We call task structure the part of a scenario fixed before any choice of agent or meta-operator, namely the environment family and evaluation distribution of the macro-environment ( Sec. II-A ) without its goal sampler, together with the interfaces through which a loop resets, replicates, or inspects its environments, a property being task-structural when replacing the agent and its three meta-operators leaves it unchanged. Task-structural resources decide whether a mechanism that presupposes them is defined or affordable, whereas capabilities of the agent’s model and artifacts authored before the loop, the two other kinds a mechanism can presuppose, hold on every target. Resources by Scenario. Three task-structural resources recur across the scenarios and are held unequally, output equivalence, checkable outcomes, and resettable rollouts.
Output equivalence, held by short discrete answers but not by images or action trajectories, defines majority voting without converting outputs into answers.
Checkable outcomes give each newly posed or reweighted goal its own reference outside the agent, coming from keys, generation programs, state scripts, or screen judges [ 152 , 64 , 115 , 14 , 316 , 317 , 318 , 319 ] .
Resettable, parallelizable rollouts let group sampling scale, at the price of emulator and virtual-machine instances where episodes run in software environments [ 14 , 125 ] .
Reasoning holds all three, navigation checkable outcomes and bought rollouts, generation free rollouts with checks only on program-checkable sub-families [ 195 , 191 ] , and manipulation checks and bought rollouts only in simulation [ 62 ] , hardware episodes costing physical time [ 19 , 21 ] .
The verdicts turn chiefly on output equivalence and checkable outcomes, rollouts entering them only through K ¯ \bar{K} , which decides whether world models are redundant.
The ceilings of Figure 3 reflect these resources only in part, navigation keeping a low K ¯ \bar{K} under per-step latency despite resettable rollouts and sharing a high F ¯ \bar{F} with reasoning, which alone holds output equivalence.
Portability is therefore derived from the resources of each scenario, not read off its quadrant. Observability and Online Checks. Two further conditions decide whether a held resource serves the loop, the observability of the outcome, which task structure fixes, and an online check built on the resource, which the loop’s design supplies or omits.
The outcome is observable where it appears in the agent’s observations or in a check available to the loop, a condition that every mechanism judging or checking it presupposes.
An online check , a check built on a resource that runs inside the loop, is the only route by which the resource lowers the realized cost of a trade-off, and loops run one wherever answer keys, execution results, or simulator outcomes are available, training-free methods checking their writes against them [ 146 , 201 , 290 ] .
Holding a resource does not ensure such a check, task streams, open prompts, and self-chosen practice tasks leaving none even in navigation, as in WebCoach’s task stream [ 163 ] .
The frozen acting model then judges its own episodes and writes the update, as in ManimAgent [ 256 ] , Mobile-Agent-E [ 162 ] , and SEAR [ 20 ] , and an error of judgment enters the update unchecked, one simulated SEAR run saving a self-built verifier wrong on 91% of later rollouts.
A loop without an online check thus gives up a resource that its scenario holds. VIII-C Trade-offs of Self-Evolving Mechanisms Table XIII recasts the mechanisms of the scenario chapters as trade-offs, in which an agent-realized meta-operator raises a factor by supplying what the agent records, proposes, judges, or writes in place of a component fixed before the loop.
The goal sampler supplies the agent’s success record or its own proposals in place of a fixed goal distribution, the feedback function the agent’s judgment in place of an answer key or a checker, and the evolution operator the agent’s authorship in place of an update rule written in advance.
Each trade-off pays what the supplied output lacks relative to the replaced component and presupposes a resource that is task-structural, a capability of the agent’s model, or an artifact authored before the loop.
Of its three elements, the factor raised, the resource presupposed, and the price paid, only the first two enter the portability verdicts. Experience Generation. The mechanisms of experience generation raise 𝔼 ⁡ [ I 𝒜 k ] \mathbb{E}[I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}] , scarce in every scenario, and differ in what they give up to raise it, with a learned world model as the one exception.
Success-conditioned allocation reweights a fixed task set, giving up coverage of the tasks it down-weights and presupposing checkable outcomes on that set.
Created goals, posed by the agent or generated from its record, extend the factor beyond a fixed pool but give up the answer key or checking script of each pooled goal, presupposing in its place an outcome that something other than their generator can check or answers that a vote can compare.
Program-checkable goal families keep the check by creating goals only where a program answers or renders them, as in VQS [ 64 ] and RSI in UMMs [ 191 ] , giving up coverage of 𝒢 \mathcal{G} outside the family.
A learned world model, the one mechanism that replaces a component of the environment and not a fixed component of a meta-operator, instead raises K k K_{k} , replacing the environment dynamics P P with imagined rollouts that K ¯ \bar{K} does not bound and presupposing dynamics learnable over short horizons, the trade-off that model-based reinforcement learning has analyzed from Dyna [ 320 ] to MBPO [ 321 ] .
It enters the loop through V ν V_{\nu} , its imagined outcomes scored by a reward or value head [ 25 , 299 ] or by the agent’s own judge [ 298 ] , and model error thus reaches F ν F_{\nu} . Feedback Acquisition. Each feedback mechanism pays for the substituted judgment in fidelity, its gain depending on the evaluator it replaces, and the mechanisms fall into three groups by the kind of resource they presuppose, the distinction on which their verdicts turn.
The self-consistency vote raises F ν F_{\nu} without keys at a price of several rollouts per goal and presupposes output equivalence together with a prior concentrated on the true answer.
Self-judgment and generator-confidence rewards raise F ν F_{\nu} without checkers and presuppose only capabilities of the agent’s model, a generation-verification gap or the generator’s confidence, which hold on every target, the gap pricing these rewards without deciding where they port.
Verifiers refit on the agent’s trajectories and frozen self-derived estimators raise L ν L_{\nu} besides F ν F_{\nu} and presuppose an artifact authored before the loop, a labeled seed, a reference set, or demonstrations. Update Consolidation. Update consolidation contributes one trade-off, episode reuse through memory, skills, or harnesses, which raises 𝔼 ⁡ [ I 𝒜 k ] \mathbb{E}[I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}] on goals initially out of reach and, where a written library carries beyond 𝒢 k \mathcal{G}_{k} , also G ζ G_{\zeta} , paying in exposure to the writer’s errors.
It presupposes either the writer’s generation-verification gap, a capability held on every target, or an online check that gates the writes, as keys and validation sets do in training-free reasoning loops [ 146 , 225 , 222 ] .
Weight updates under a rule fixed before the loop supply no agent authorship and enter the trade-offs only through the feedback they consume. VIII-D Costs and Alternative Sources The evidence on the price paid, the trade-off element the verdicts leave out, serves the account in two ways, setting the cost of the conditional routes the verdicts open and bearing on whether a factor other than scarcity and resources could produce the asymmetry.
The comparisons below each stay inside one paper, bearing on a mechanism’s gain and, where measured, its cost.
Degradation, the rest of the cost, is left aside, its evidence too uneven for cross-scenario comparison. Generation-Verification Gap. The generation-verification gap prices a trade-off wherever the agent’s own judgment serves as the reference, as a capability that holds on every target ( Sec. VIII-C ), and remains inferred in multimodal loops.
Introduced for language models, the gap is the improvement a model obtains by filtering or reweighting its own generations with its own verification [ 322 ] .
We extend the term by separating a task-level component, the difference in difficulty between verifying and generating on a task family, from a model-level component, the difference between the verification and generation accuracy of the agent’s own model on the same goals.
The model-level component remains unestimated, multimodal audits reporting only the verification side [ 64 , 256 , 281 ] , and backbone priors would confound it even once measured ( Sec. III-E ).
We infer the task-level component to be large in GUI and Web navigation, where a final screen can be inspected without replaying the steps that reach it, and small in open-prompt generation, where judging whether an image satisfies a prompt calls on the compositional understanding that producing it requires, a difference that makes the cost of self-judgment vary across scenarios even where its verdict does not. Evaluator Fidelity. The feedback ablations order evaluators by fidelity and thereby price the conditional routes that substitute a judge for a missing check.
Two ablations, both in generation, compare a verifier built on the agent’s own judgment with the evaluator it replaces, a frozen self-rater against a program check in one and a co-trained reward model against a learned preference scorer in the other.
In RSI in UMMs, training on the better of two images as rated by the frozen starting model gains 0.5 points, against 8.6 points with execution-verified charts for the same requests and steps [ 191 ] .
The reward model that The Image as Its Own Reward [ 245 ] co-trains against reference images matches the PickScore of Flow-GRPO [ 195 ] trained on PickScore and wins 70.0% of the authors’ human image-quality comparisons against it, a model that the same raters place below its base.
A verifier built on the agent’s own judgment thus falls below a program check and can exceed a learned preference scorer, an ordering whose two ends come from different verifiers, one frozen and one co-trained, and which the other scenarios leave untested.
The ordering would be contradicted if such a verifier outperformed a keyed or programmatic check at a matched budget on fixed goals. Goal-Mechanism Gains. Goal mechanisms gain in all four scenarios with one reported exception, consistent with a scarcity of 𝔼 ⁡ [ I 𝒜 k ] \mathbb{E}[I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}] that gives this factor no part in the asymmetry.
Removing allocation hurts both scenarios that ablate it, MobileRL [ 17 ] losing 6.3 points of AndroidWorld success without its failure curriculum filter and Policy-Level RSI [ 296 ] raising its ManiSkill failure rate from 2.34% to 3.36% under random scene selection.
Created goals lead untrained, random, or frozen-source generators in VisPlay [ 5 ] , RATs [ 302 ] , ACuRL [ 15 ] , and RSIgen [ 260 ] , and trail the NNetNav curriculum by 1.3 points in one SEAgent setting [ 16 ] .
Within a program-checkable family the error-driven chart distribution of RSI in UMMs leads a fixed distribution with the same number of verified charts by 2.8 to 4.1 points in every round [ 191 ] .
These comparisons come from the authors of each method and may overstate the gain, yet their agreement across the four scenarios leaves the verdicts on goal mechanisms to turn on the resources they presuppose. Writer Dependence. The gain of reuse depends on the writer, a model-level dependence that travels with the agent to every target and adds no asymmetry of its own.
Authored writes outperform skill-free or raw alternatives in two navigation ablations, EvoSkill-GUI [ 281 ] gaining 6.7 points on MobileWorld over pass@3 without skills and Agent S [ 278 ] 7.7 points on an OSWorld subset over full trajectories in place of its self-evaluator’s summaries.
SEAR [ 20 ] , holding the scenario fixed and varying only the writer, finds the sign of this gain reversed, GPT-5.6-sol on RoboLab dropping from 46.9% without a library to 42.4% with its own, beyond the spread of three runs, and rising to 60.6% with one that Claude Opus 5 wrote, a loss the authors trace to object-location recipes with few grasp checks.
Whether such content tracks the generation-verification gap of the writer’s model is untested, and a library that gained the same whoever wrote it would contradict the dependence on the writer. a b b b c a b b b a b b b e e e e f f f f K ¯ \bar{K} F ¯ \bar{F} Fig. 4 : Directed portability on the positioning matrix. VIII-E Directed Portability across Scenario Pairs The two directions of each scenario pair receive verdicts of different kinds, a difference traced to which factors are scarce and which task-structural resources and conditions hold.
The direction from X X to Y Y collects the mechanisms portable or conditional in X X that on Y Y are redundant or rank lower in the order portable, conditional, blocked, and reversing the pair exchanges both the mechanisms carried and the conditions they meet. A mechanism whose verdict does not fall belongs to neither direction by construction, and the asymmetry accordingly concerns the kind of obstacle a direction meets, not how many mechanisms transfer. Table XIV assigns per-target verdicts by the rules below. Verdict Rules. The rules apply in a fixed order, observability first, a mechanism that judges or checks an unobservable outcome being blocked whatever its other conditions, then scarcity, which decides redundancy, and resources last, which decide between portable and conditional.
A mechanism is redundant where the factor it raises is not scarce, judged at the ceiling or, where the loop gives up a resource, at the realized value, as for self-labeled questions without keys and task streams without online checks. 𝔼 ⁡ [ I 𝒜 k ] \mathbb{E}[I_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}] and G ζ G_{\zeta} , neither bounded by a ceiling, count as scarce everywhere ( Sec. VIII-A ), and L ν L_{\nu} as not scarce where a single action fixes L ν = 1 L_{\nu}\!=\!1 .
Otherwise a mechanism is portable where the target holds the resource it presupposes and conditional where an adaptation, such as converting outputs into answers or adding an external judge, or a sub-family supplies it, a missing resource admitting such a route in the surveyed targets wherever the outcome is observable.
Capabilities of the agent’s model and artifacts authored before the loop count as held everywhere ( Sec. VIII-B ).
Where the outcome is unobservable, observability caps the verdict and every mechanism that judges or checks it is blocked , even those presupposing only the agent’s model, whereas low evaluator accuracy only raises the cost of a conditional route.
A portable verdict states that a precondition holds, not that a transfer improves the target loop. Redundant Verdicts. Redundant verdicts fall on world models where rollouts are cheap and on feedback mechanisms where an external reference already supplies every factor they raise.
Reasoning and generation keep K ¯ \bar{K} high through a single decoding or sampling pass, leaving world models redundant there.
The vote, self-judgment, and generator-confidence rewards are redundant on reasoning questions with keys or program-computed answers and on navigation goals whose outcome a check inspects online.
Self-judging navigation loops such as Agent S [ 278 ] and Mobile-Agent-E [ 162 ] , by contrast, run on task streams or practice goals whose screen-visible outcomes no check inspects, leaving F ν F_{\nu} scarce at the realized value and self-judgment portable.
Refit verifiers and frozen estimators are redundant only on these reasoning questions, where one action fixes L ν = 1 L_{\nu}\!=\!1 , multi-step navigation and manipulation keeping L ν L_{\nu} scarce. Conditional Verdicts. Conditional verdicts arise where the target holds a presupposed task-structural resource only on a sub-family or through an adaptation.
Allocation and program-checkable families are conditional where checks cover only a sub-family, as in generation [ 11 , 191 ] and on hardware, where an external judge grades a task library [ 21 ] or a scripted scene check covers a fixed family [ 323 ] .
Created goals, checkable in reasoning and navigation, find a check in generation only through image questions, rendered charts, or external judges [ 40 , 191 , 260 ] .
On hardware, agent-posed goals stay in simulation [ 302 , 20 ] , and the external judge of before-and-after images under which SEAR practices given tasks offers a route where success shows as object motion, a case FailBench finds nearly solved [ 324 ] .
World models find learnable dynamics on hardware only over short horizons [ 24 ] and in navigation only over text-rendered web states, as in DynaWeb [ 325 ] .
Outside the discrete answers of reasoning, the vote finds output equivalence only through questions that turn outputs into comparable answers, as ASG poses about generated images [ 40 ] , a conversion untested on final screens and camera frames. Blocked Verdicts. Blocked verdicts arise where the outcome of a goal appears neither in the agent’s observations nor in any check available to the loop, as for background-state goals in navigation that no reachable screen shows and no script inspects.
Contact-rich assembly is the candidate sub-family on hardware, where no judge in FailBench exceeds 0.60 balanced accuracy against 0.77 for the best judge overall, a result consistent with frames that omit the contact on which success depends, although the authors also name task-specific knowledge and precision [ 324 ] .
On such goals the vote and self-judgment have nothing to judge, recorded actions showing what the agent did and not whether the state changed, created goals have nothing to check them, and success-conditioned allocation has no success record to read, a block reaching model-only mechanisms as well as resource-dependent ones.
Refit verifiers and frozen estimators keep the progress that visible steps show, and episode reuse the writes that need no judgment of success, both becoming conditional there, whereas generator-confidence rewards, which never judge the outcome, stay portable at their cost in fidelity, a verdict certifying only that their precondition holds while their fidelity on outcomes that nothing observes stays unknown.
No reported instance falls in a blocked cell, leaving the rules consistent yet unsupported. Asymmetry by Target. The kind of verdict that a direction meets is fixed chiefly by its target, and the two directions of a pair differ wherever their targets differ in scarcity, resources, or observability.
Checkable outcomes act on both sides of the pair of navigation and generation, the judges of generation becoming redundant on navigation goals that a check inspects online and the created goals of navigation becoming conditional on the open prompts of generation.
Reasoning and manipulation show the pattern most sharply, the vote and the goal mechanisms carried from reasoning becoming conditional on hardware and, program-checkable families aside, blocked where contact decides success, while the world models and estimators of manipulation become redundant on reasoning questions with keys or program-computed answers ( Table XIV ).
More broadly, directions into reasoning meet only redundancy, directions into generation and hardware chiefly conditional goal mechanisms and votes, and where the outcome is unobservable, navigation and hardware block goal mechanisms and judges from every source, a verdict that depends on the target alone ( Figure 4 ). Structural Source. Taken together, we interpret the structural source of the asymmetry as two scenario properties that the directions of a pair meet in different combinations, the scarcity that K ¯ \bar{K} and F ¯ \bar{F} fix, read at the realized value where a loop gives up a resource, and the unequal task structure, comprising its resources and the observability of its outcomes.
Scarcity decides redundancy, task-structural resources decide between portable and conditional verdicts, and observability alone decides blocking.
Observability is also the one task-structural condition that bounds mechanisms presupposing only the agent’s model.
Matrix positions anticipate most verdicts, failing where output equivalence or observability decides them, as for the vote and background-state goals in navigation.
Still untested, the verdicts would fall to a gain over an equal-budget control for an unadapted mechanism lacking its resource, one raising a non-scarce factor, or one judging unobservable outcomes. are evaluated on task-solving capability, on self-evolving capability, and through the judges and reward models that supply their feedback, and identifies the measurements whose absence leaves our account untestable. IX-A Task-Solving Capability Task-solving capability, the expected return 𝒥 k \mathcal{J}_{k} ( Eq. 6 ) that an agent attains on the goals of a scenario with its components fixed, is measured on task-specific benchmarks that fix a goal set, any environment that rollouts need, and usually a scorer ( Table XV ).
A reported score estimates 𝒥 k \mathcal{J}_{k} only insofar as the goal set represents μ 𝒢 \mu_{\mathcal{G}} and the scorer agrees with R R , and audits find both conditions broken.
The goal set fails through memorization, Sphinx-X-MoE reaching 43.6% on MMMU [ 327 ] without images, 17.9 points above its language-model backbone [ 359 ] and vision-language-action models above 90% on LIBERO [ 348 ] falling to 0.0% under the perturbations of LIBERO-PRO [ 349 ] .
The scorer fails through divergence and exploits, GenEval [ 334 ] deviating from human judgment by up to 17.7 points [ 360 ] and BenchJack [ 361 ] reaching near-perfect scores on most of ten agent benchmarks without solving a task.
A loop that trains with the scorer it is evaluated by, as Reflection-VLA [ 304 ] does on LIBERO, can write memorized goals and scorer errors into the agent, a self-evolving agent thus being more exposed to these failures than the static models and agents that the audits examine. IX-B Self-Evolving Capability Self-evolving capability, the ability to raise 𝒥 k \mathcal{J}_{k} over the cycles of a loop, is measured by self-evolution benchmarks that fix the goals, the environment, any feedback, and the schedule in which the agent attempts and is tested on goals ( Table XVI ), whereas loops on task-specific benchmarks report 𝒥 k \mathcal{J}_{k} by round or only at the endpoints.
Text-only benchmarks apply the same protocol to memory, lifelong-learning, and harness-evolution streams and to professional workflows [ 362 , 363 , 364 , 365 , 366 , 367 , 368 , 369 , 370 , 371 , 372 , 373 , 374 , 375 , 376 , 377 , 378 ] , whereas benchmarks in which the agent remembers supplied histories [ 379 , 380 , 381 ] , learns from supplied demonstrations or labeled streams [ 382 , 383 ] , or is scored without updating itself [ 384 ] fall outside this measurement, leaving no update that follows from the agent’s own outcomes.
Independent scores across rounds trace the increment Δ ​ 𝒥 = 𝒥 k + 1 − 𝒥 k \Delta\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}_{k+1}-\mathcal{J}_{k} , cumulative scores mix it with resampling, endpoint comparisons give only the total gain, and re-tests of earlier goals and held-out goals bear on retention S ζ S_{\zeta} and generalization G ζ G_{\zeta} .
A gain counts as evidence of the capability only to the extent that controls separate the agent’s experience from added computation, repeated attempts, writing itself, and run variance, as in MemGUI-Bench [ 385 ] , where retries alone raise M3A by 14.9 points while removing the long-term memory of Agent-S2 at the same three attempts costs it 20.0 points.
Whether the check reaches the agent also shapes the gain, conditions that withhold it lowering gains or leaving them mixed [ 386 , 387 ] , with late-round successes in PhysRSI-eval falling from 15 to 5 of 36 without the grader [ 388 ] , whereas the protocols marked † return the check, a gain under them possibly including its exploitation, as exposing a simulator’s success criteria to embodied agents yields gains that vanish once the criteria change slightly [ 389 ] .
These benchmarks measure mechanisms only on goals whose outcomes are checked, away from the goals where Table XIV sets verdicts by missing checks or unobservable outcomes, and cover navigation and manipulation, on hardware only in SEAR’s five tasks [ 20 ] , with reasoning and generation covered only by Flex-RSI [ 390 ] , which omits navigation. IX-C Judges and Reward Models Where no answer key or program checks an output, a self-evolving agent realizes V ν V_{\nu} through a judge or a reward model, whose preferences f ζ f_{\zeta} writes into 𝒜 k + 1 \mathcal{A}_{k+1} while the loop observes no R R , moving the policy toward outputs that the scorer overrates.
A scorer that shares the updated parameters or is refit moves with the policy as well.
Evaluations compare these scorers with a reference outside the loop ( Table XVII ) and bear on the realized fidelity F ν ( k ) F_{\nu}^{(k)} only insofar as their outputs and scorer resemble those in the loop.
On fixed outputs, agreement stays moderate and falls off the training distribution, the strongest judges reaching 62.9%–72% accuracy [ 391 , 392 , 393 , 285 ] , reward models dropping to near chance on pairs outside their training data [ 135 , 394 ] , and a log that reports success misleading judges on failed clips [ 395 ] .
Optimization exposes further errors, reward hacking reaching 48.1% for VQA policies [ 396 ] and a judge passing repairs that humans mostly reject [ 395 ] , although a fixed screen judge stays accurate on the policy trained against it [ 15 ] and fixed-output accuracy largely orders scorers by the downstream results of policies optimized against them [ 142 , 397 ] .
References that check outcomes reach open-ended outputs only through single-property interventions [ 398 ] , leaving such outputs scored against human labels, larger models, or other reward models [ 396 , 399 , 400 ] . IX-D Measurement Gaps We list a measurement as missing only where its absence leaves one of three claims untestable, that a method’s gain comes from self-evolution, that a scenario sits where we place it on the positioning matrix, and that the portability verdicts hold, the last two specific to our account.
These measurements share the need for a reference the loop never receives, a reference returned to the agent entering its feedback V ν V_{\nu} and becoming exploitable by its updates. Attribution of Gains. Attributing a gain to self-evolution requires, in every scenario, the increment across rounds against a non-evolving baseline at a matched budget and, where the scorer that checks updates also produces the reported score, a reference withheld from the loop.
Four self-evolution benchmarks run this baseline ( Table XVI ), Flex-RSI among them with harness edits raising the test score in six of seven paired runs [ 390 ] , as does RATs [ 302 ] , whereas the other comparisons reported in reasoning and generation, such as V-Zero against supervised GRPO [ 7 ] , state no matched budget.
On hardware the budget also counts the human resets that 𝒞 1 \mathcal{C}_{1} admits, which Find Something You Can’t Do records as scene recoveries [ 21 ] , lest an evolving arm draw on more of them than its baseline.
The withheld reference, such as the test split that Flex-RSI scores once after each run [ 390 ] , separates the gain from exploitation of the scorer, as for the protocols marked † , and, where training and reported goals coincide, from memorization.
Agreement on fixed outputs ranks scorers without bounding that exploitation, and the audits in Table XVII cover parameter updates and single-output repair, leaving exploitation through memory and tool writes untested. Minecraft frames, planner state stage verifiers b † M source to reset target – – related tasks model calls, env steps Scenario Placement. Placing a scenario depends only on whether each ceiling is low or high, and a misplacement would merely move verdicts into or out of the redundant class.
Rollout cost in a common unit would test the split on K ¯ \bar{K} , yet the reported units do not convert, ranging from tokens in MemGUI-Bench to hours of robot time in Self-Improving EFMs [ 19 ] .
The split on F ¯ \bar{F} remains inferred, the evaluations of Table XVII defining each reference within one scenario and values taken from fixed outputs erring in either direction.
A higher F ¯ \bar{F} than we place in generation or on hardware would make the feedback mechanisms there redundant, and for generation the references on optimized outputs reduce to human labels in a simulated repair loop [ 395 ] and, under retraining, other reward models [ 408 ] . Verdict Testing. Testing a verdict of Table XIV means running the matched-budget comparison in the target scenario with the mechanism moved unadapted, on goals outside the self-evolution benchmarks wherever the verdict turns on missing checks or unobservable outcomes.
A conditional verdict also needs the adapted arm, and a blocked verdict an outcome check held outside the loop, one available to the loop making the outcome observable.
Where a loop gives up a resource, the verdict rests on realized F ν F_{\nu} , whose test needs the policy optimized against a scorer that changes with it, a joint condition that Table XVII checks against a hidden key only in text [ 409 ] .
The reported transfers fall short of such a test, ASG [ 40 ] bringing voting to generation without an unadapted arm, failure-conditioned curricula moving from the text-only WebRL [ 410 ] to screen-based loops [ 16 , 15 ] only within navigation, and SEAR starting its hardware tasks from an empty library instead of the one written in simulation.
The verdicts thus remain untested under a shared protocol. TABLE XVII : Evaluations of multimodal judges and reward models. Fixed, Optimized, and Retrained mark scoring of fixed outputs, of outputs trained or selected against the scorer, and across retraining of the scorer.

policy’s model chat responses human rankings ✓ ✓ X Open Challenges This section discusses four open problems: reference construction, self-derived verification, and cross-substrate transfer, which inform our account of the cross-scenario asymmetry, along with safety under autonomy, which bears on the operational definition of self-evolution. X-A Reference Construction Reference construction, the building of a substitute reference for an outcome absent from both the agent’s observations and every check available to the loop, decides whether the blocked verdicts can be lifted, as for background-state goals in navigation and possibly contact-rich assembly on hardware ( Sec. VIII-E ), and the four reported routes each succeed only in part.
Converting outputs into answers [ 40 ] needs an equivalence relation that screens and action trajectories lack, and a program written together with the goal covers only goal families with a closed-form success condition, EvoCUA resolving by hand the synthesized tasks on which its two checks disagree [ 416 ] .
A learned world model adds its own prediction error, its dynamics learned on hardware only over short horizons [ 24 ] and in navigation only over text-rendered states [ 325 ] , while a vision-language judge reads camera frames that do not record contact forces [ 324 ] .
Whether a loop can construct such a reference without a human inside the loop thus remains open, an affirmative answer turning the blocked verdicts into conditional ones that depend on how the reference is built and a negative one making the block a property of the outcome itself. X-B Self-Derived Verification Self-derived verification, the scoring of outcomes or steps by a verifier derived from the agent’s own model or trajectories, is adopted wherever no check scores an outcome or a check returns one verdict per long episode, and its reliability turns on how far the verifier depends on the policy.
Verifiers that change with the policy drift or inherit its errors, confidence rewards collapsing in ablated configurations of SOLACE [ 9 ] and IRIS [ 129 ] and step reward models trained on the agent’s trajectories inheriting its mistakes [ 135 , 144 ] .
Lagged, co-trained, and frozen verifiers, such as the moving-average teacher of UniEvo-VL [ 263 ] , the initial model in PRISM [ 247 ] , and progress heads frozen before reinforcement learning [ 19 , 145 ] , depend on the policy less or not at all, trading a moving bias for one fixed by their source, a trade that no report compares across degrees of dependence.
Whether any degree of dependence keeps a verifier consistent with R R once the policy leaves the support on which the verifier was formed remains open, an affirmative answer making reliability a choice of dependence and supplying step-level credit to long episodes, and a negative one turning the low F ¯ \bar{F} of generation and hardware and the scarcity of L ν L_{\nu} in navigation and manipulation into structural limits. X-C Cross-Substrate Transfer Cross-substrate transfer, the carrying of what a loop writes, whether a skill library, a harness, or a policy, to another reader, platform, or embodiment once the missing resources are supplied, underlies the conditional verdicts for hardware targets, and the reported evidence leaves it unsettled.
Across platforms, zero-shot transfer is reported only for the fixed loop GUI-HARVEST, whose harness evolved on OSWorld raises GPT-5 by 13.87 points on WindowsAgentArena [ 286 ] .
Across simulators and action interfaces, the code policies of SEAR lose little while its VLA policies lose most of their success, the former falling from 33.3% to 28.7% and the latter from 43.1% to 10.2% [ 20 ] .
Across readers, the same WebCoach memory lowers a 7B agent and raises larger ones [ 163 ] , in line with the writer dependence of Sec. VIII-D .
No report carries a write across the simulation-to-hardware boundary, SEAR starting its hardware tasks from an empty library ( Sec. IX-D ).
Whether a write takes effect across readers, platforms, and embodiments, the simulation-to-hardware boundary included, without new experience accordingly remains open, an affirmative answer loosening the conditional verdicts for hardware targets and a negative one making the substrate itself a missing resource there. X-D Safety under Autonomy Autonomy ( 𝒞 1 \mathcal{C}_{1} ) excludes human veto from the loop, leaving the loop itself to preserve safety across modifications, as the first of the three laws proposed for self-evolving agents requires [ 28 ] , and the reported evidence shows safety eroding along each path of self-modification examined.
Degradation is reported along the model, memory, tool, and workflow paths, on text and code except for one screen-based case, SEAgent, whose unsafe completion rate on RiOSWorld rises from 39.5% to 55.3% [ 417 ] .
Experience accumulated only on benign tasks in text-rendered web and embodied environments raises attack success on later high-risk tasks [ 418 ] , and safety-oriented loops such as FailBank [ 419 ] draw their proposals from a teacher fixed before the loop, leaving untouched the loops in which the agent supplies its own feedback.
Whether a loop can preserve a safety property across cycles while no human input reaches its goals, feedback, updates, or actions remains open, an affirmative answer showing autonomy and safety need not trade off under 𝒞 1 \mathcal{C}_{1} and a negative one making the trade-off a consequence of the definition, common to all scenarios. XI Conclusion Summary. This survey investigates the structural source of cross-scenario asymmetry in mechanism portability across four scenarios.
Through the three-stage self-evolving loop, we place the four scenarios in a positioning matrix spanned by ceilings on rollout count and feedback fidelity, and use fixed loops to separate scenario-imposed limits from those incurred by self-evolving design.
We then recast each mechanism as a trade-off that raises a scarce factor by presupposing a task-structural resource, and attribute the asymmetry to the different combinations of scarce factors and resources that the two directions of a scenario pair encounter.
Inferred from single-scenario evidence, the resulting portability verdicts are testable preconditions, not empirical guarantees. Sec. IX identifies the measurements that would test them, and Sec. X discusses the problems that existing mechanisms leave open. Limitations. Although this survey characterizes the cross-scenario asymmetry through an explicit definition, framework, and set of verdict rules, it has the following limitations.
First, inclusion requires the agent to realize at least one of the goal sampler, the feedback function, or the evolution operator, a criterion that confines the scenario patterns to the self-evolving subset and admits fixed loops only as controls. The provenance assignments and the grouping axes of the scenario taxonomies were made by a single coder, the axes after the binding constraints had been identified, and the consolidation profiles partly reflect which scenarios report retention and generalization at all.
Second, the quadrant placement rests on single-scenario estimates of rollout cost and feedback fidelity that do not separate the pretraining coverage of the backbone from the task structure, and the verdicts are derived from rules, not observed in transfer experiments.
Third, the three-stage decomposition is one of several possible stage boundaries, and credit assignment, treated here as a refinement of feedback acquisition, could instead form a separate stage.
Finally, the surveyed methods center on image, video, screen, and robot-camera inputs, leaving loops with audio, tactile, or force feedback unexamined, and later work, particularly in embodied manipulation, may revise the placements and verdicts. Instructions for reporting errors We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
      support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
      methods listed below: Click the "Report Issue" ( ) button, located in the page header. Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report. Our team has already identified the following issues . We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
      may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
      should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
      all. Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a list of packages that need conversion , and welcome developer contributions . We gratefully acknowledge support from our major funders , member institutions , ,
        and all contributors. About · Help · Contact · Subscribe · Copyright · Privacy · Accessibility · Operational Status (opens in new tab)
